SECTION 3: That the Director of Revenue shall cause to 
be printed and bound copies of this Code for the use of 
Jefferson County and he shall supervise the publication 


A-149 


of said Code and he shall read all of the proofs, correct 


all manifest errors and compare the printed book with 
this ordinance. 


SECTION 4: (a) The term “person” as used in this 
Code shall include an individual, a corporation, a 
partnership, an association, a stock company, a business 
trust, an unincorporated organization, and any other en- 
tity. 

(b) In all cases where the amount of license tax is 
dependent upon the amount of business done, amount 
of sales, receipts, value of furniture and other equip- 
ment, stock, capital invested, number of helpers or em- 
ployees, amount of space occupied, or any factor or fac- 
tors whatsoever either of the same kind as the foregoing 
enumerations or of a different kind therefrom, it shall be 
the duty of the person subject to such tax to render to 
the Director of Revenue, on or prior to the date when 
such tax becomes due, a sworn statement showing the 
total business done, amount of sales, receipts, stock, 
value of furniture and other equipment, capital invested, 
number of helpers or employees, amount of space occu- 
pied, or other factor or factors herein above mentioned, 
one or several, as the case may require for the 
ascertainment of the classification of such person for li- 
cense taxation purposes and the correct amount of li- 
cense tax to which he is subject. 

(c) Upon receipt of license tax based upon the classi- 
fication of such person as shown by such sworn state- 
ment, the Director of Revenue shall issue appropriate 
license, but the issuance and grant thereof is expressly 
conditional upon the truth and accuracy of such sworn 
statement and the same shall be revocable by the Com- 
mission of Jefferson County as inadvertently and mistak- 
enly granted and issued whenever it shall be made to 
satisfactorily appear to said Commission that the said 


A-150 


sworn statement is false or incorrect or that the holder of 
such license has not paid to the Jefferson County Com- 
mission the correct amount of license tax. 

(d) Upon demand by the Director of Revenue, or his 
authorized deputy, auditor or representative, it shall be 
the duty of any person holding a license from Jefferson 
County to throw open to said Director or his authorized 
deputy, auditor or representative, during the business 
hours, all portions of his place or places of business for 
the purpose of enabling said Director of Revenue, or his 
authorized deputy, auditor or representative, to ascertain 
and gain such information as may be necessary or conve- 
nient for determination of the proper classification of 
such license holder for license taxation purposes and de- 
termination of the correct amount of license tax to which 
such person is subject, and, upon demand by the Direc- 
tor of Revenue, or his authorized deputy, auditor or rep- 
resentative, it shall also be the duty of any person holding 
a license from the County to furnish such Director of 
Revenue or his authorized deputy, auditor or representa- 
tive, all such information as may be required for determi- 
nation of the proper classification of such license holder 
for license taxation purposes and determination of the 
correct amount of license tax to which such person is 
subject, and to that end it shall be the duty of such per- 
son to submit to the Director of Revenue, or his autho- 
rized deputy, auditor or representative, for inspection 
and examination, during reasonable business hours, at 
such person's place of business, in the County, all books 
of account, invoices, papers, reports, and memoranda 
containing entries showing amount of purchases, sales 
receipts, inventory and other information from which 
the correct license tax classification of such person may 
be ascertained and the correct amount of license tax to 
which he is subject be determined including exhibition 
of bank deposit books, and bank statements, copies of 


A-151 


Sales Tax Reports to the State of Alabama and Jefferson 
County, copies of Alabama Income Tax Reports and Fed- 
eral Income Tax Reports. It shall be unlawful for any 
license holder, or for any servant, agent or employee of 
such license holder, to fail or refuse to perform any duty 
herein imposed upon such license holder, or to obstruct 
or interfere with the Director of Revenue, or his autho- 
rized deputy, auditor, or representative, in obtaining in- 
formation necessary or convenient for determination of 
the proper license classification of such license holder. 
(e) Where the amount of license required to be paid 
for engaging in any business is graduated according to 
the amount of business, or the amount of gross receipts, 
gross purchases, gross sales, or according to the amount 
or value of stock on hand, and where a maximum flat 
sum which shall be paid as a license is provided for en- 
gaging in such business, any person engaging in such 
business shall be liable for the maximum license pro- 
vided for engaging in such business, unless he has in his 
possession the records, books, papers, and reports neces- 
sary to determine the classification to which he belongs, 
and unless he exhibits the same for inspection as re- 
quired by this section, and unless the same disclose that 
the person belongs in a classification lower than the clas- 
sification required to pay the maximum license. Where 
the amount of license required to be paid for engaging 
in any business is graduated according to the amount of 
business, or the amount of gross receipts, gross pur- 
chases, gross sales, or according to the amount of value 
of stock on hand, and where no maximum flat sum 
which shall be paid for engaging in such business is pro- 
vided for, then in such event if the holder of a license of 
the County has not in his possession or under his control 
in Jefferson County true and intelligible books of ac- 
counts, invoices, papers, reports or memoranda correctly 
reflecting the data and information necessary for deter- 


A-152 


mination of the classification to which he belongs for 
assessment of the correct amount of license tax, or if, 
having in his possession or under his control such books, 
invoices, papers, reports and/or memoranda, he shall 
fail or refuse to submit and exhibit the same for inspec- 
tion and examination as required by this section, or if he 
shall fail or refuse to throw open any portion of his place 
or places of business to the Director of Revenue, or his 
authorized deputy, auditor or representative, as in this 
section requires, then and in either of said events it shall 
be the duty of the Director of Revenue to ascertain the 
license tax classification of such person from the best 
information and data obtainable and to assess against any 
such person the correct license tax according to the clas- 
sification thus ascertained. When the classification of 
such person, and the amount of license tax due from 
him shall have been ascertained and fixed by the Direc- 
tor of Revenue, it shall be the duty of such Director to 
mail to such person notice of his determination and to 
demand of such person immediate payment of the 
amount of tax ascertained to be due from him plus pen- 
alties but less any amount such person may have thereto- 
fore paid upon the tax due from him. If the amount of 
tax demanded be not paid within ten days of the mailing 
of such demand the Director of Revenue shall notify 
such person by registered mail that at a time and place 
specified in said notice, he will apply to the Commission 
of Jefferson County for revocation of such person's li- 
cense. At the time and place so specified such person 
may appear before the Commission in person and by 
counsel, one or both, and show cause, if any there be, 
why said license should not be revoked. Upon any such 
hearing, and for all other purposes, the decision and 
determination of the Director of Revenue as to classifica- 
tion and amount of license tax due shall be deemed cor- 


A-153 


rect and final, unless and until it shall be satisfactorily 
shown that such decision and determination is incorrect. 
(f) It shall be unlawful for any person connected with 
the administration of this section to divulge any informa- 
tion obtained by him in the course of inspection and 
examination of the books, papers, reports and memo- 
randa of the taxpayer made pursuant to the provision of 
this exception, except to the Jefferson County Commis- 
sion, the Director of Revenue, the County Attorney, or 
others connected with the administration of this section. 

(g) It shall be the right and duty of the Director of 

Revenue, his deputy or inspector to inspect from time to 
time during reasonable business hours all records, books 
and other papers or documents, as well as merchandise 
itself that is placed or stored in public warehouses in the 
County as well as all other places where merchandise is 
stored for or by some person, firm or corporation, said 
information obtained from such inspection and investi- 
gation to be used as an aid in determining the proper 
classification of the owner of said merchandise for li- 
cense taxation and for fixing the amount of such license 
tax, and it shall be unlawful for any person, firm or cor- 
poration to impede, hinder or obstruct the Director of 
Revenue or his deputy or inspector in reasonable perfor- 
mance of his duty. 

(h) For the purpose of enabling the Director of Rev- 
enue to determine (1) whether or not there is any liabil- 
ity for payment of a license tax, (2) the proper classifica- 
tion, or (3) the correct amount of license tax due, it shall 
be the duty of any person engaged in or carrying on any 
business, vocation, occupation or profession in the 
County, upon demand of the Director of Revenue, his 
authorized deputy, auditor or representative, to permit 
inspection during business hours of all portions of his 
place or places of business, to furnish by sworn statement 
or otherwise all information requested bearing on the 


A-154 


character of the business engaged in, the amount of capi- 
tal invested therein, and the dollar amount of the busi- 
ness transacted, and to make available for inspection and 
examination during business hours all books of accounts, 
invoices, papers, reports and memoranda relating to said 
business, or showing the amount of purchases, sales, re- 
ceipts, inventory, and such other information necessary 
for a determination of tax liability, classification or 
amount of tax, as may be demanded, including bank de- 
posit books, bank statements, copies of sales tax reports 
to the State of Alabama and Jefferson County and of state 
and federal income tax returns. The above records shall 
be maintained for the three preceding calendar years 
prior to such examination. It shall be unlawful for any 
such person, or for any agent, servant or employee of 
such person, to fail or refuse to perform any duty herein 
imposed upon such person or obstruct or interfere with 
the Director of Revenue, his authorized deputy, auditor 
or representative in carrying out the purpose of this sub- 
section. Each day’s failure or refusal to perform any duty 
herein imposed shall constitute a separate offense. 


Section 5: (a): Where the amount of the license is 
based on gross annual business, gross annual receipts, 
gross receipts, gross sales or any other words of similar 
import, the terms gross annual business, gross annual 
receipts, gross receipts, gross sales or any other words of 
similar import, unless the contrary clearly appears, shall 
mean the entire receipts for the preceding calendar year 
of the business, vocation, occupation or profession en- 
gaged in, including all receipts from sales regardless of 
the place where the sale was solicited or placed where the 
contract of sale was consummated or the place of deliv- 
ery, and shall include the amount of any federal excise 
tax, or any other tax imposed on sales or services, there 
may be deducted therefrom the amounts of all state, 


A-155 


county and municipal sales and use taxes, and also, there 
may be deducted therefrom the amounts of any taxes 
collected and paid over to the State, County or Munici- 
pality. Provided however, such gross annual business, 
gross annual receipts, gross receipts, gross sales shall ex- 
clude receipts from sales with delivery being made out- 
side of the State of Alabama. Provided further that if the 
person, firm or corporation liable for a license under this 
Code, may at his or its option (1) deduct from the entire 
receipts the amount of receipts that were derived from 
the sale of merchandise or services covered by state and 
county license or licenses issued under Title 40, Chapter 
12, Code of Alabama 1975, or (2) deduct from the 
amount of the license due under this Code one-half (1/ 
2) the amount or amounts paid to the State and County 
for the licenses; excluding fees, interest and penalties: 
issued under Title 40, Chapter 12, Code of Alabama 
1975. Provided further that any person, firm or corpora- 
tion liable for a license under this Code regardless of 
whether the license is based on gross receipts or other- 
wise may deduct from the license due under this Code 
one-half (1/2) the amount or amounts paid to the State 
and County for licenses excluding fees, interest and pen- 
alties issued under Title 40, Chapter 12, Code of Ala- 
bama 1975 as amended. 

(b) Where the amount of license is based upon the 
volume of receipts, or investment in inventory, plant, 
equipment, fixtures, rolling stock, etc., in the absence of 
any other specific provision therefor, the receipts or aver- 
age investment referred to are those of such business for 
the calendar year next preceding the current license 
year, provided, however, that if said business did not op 
erate the entire next preceding calendar year, then the 
license tax shall be based upon that amount which bears 
the same relationship to the actual amount of receipts or 
investments during such preceding part year as the en- 


A-156 

tire calendar year bears to such time which said business 
was operated during such preceding part year, and pro- 
vided further that if the operation of such business did 
not commence until after December 31, 1988, or by sub- 
sequent December 31, then the license tax shall be based 
upon that amount which bears the same relationship to 
the actual amount of receipts or investments during such 
preceding part year as a 12 month period bears to such 
time which said business was operated during such pre- 
ceding part year, and provided further, that if the opera- 
tion of such business did not commence until after the 
Ist day of October, 1989, or any subsequent October 1, 
the operator thereof shall pay the minimum license for 
that license year, if the operation of the business does 
not begin until after April 1, 1990 or any subsequent 
April 1, the operator thereof shall pay one-half the mini- 
mum license for the remainder of the license year. 

(c) Where the amount of license is based upon the 
average number of employees employed in the business 
during the calendar year next preceding the current li- 
cense year and the operation of such business did not 
commence until the Ist day of such current year or 
thereafter, then the license tax for such business for such 
current license year shall be paid based on an affidavit 
given by the operator of such business, such affidavit 
shall state the average number of employees to be em- 
ployed in the business during the current year. 


Section 6: POWER TO CHANGE LICENSE: The 
adoption of this schedule of licenses shall not abridge 
the right of the Jefferson County Commission to change, 
alter, increase, decrease or revoke any of the above li- 
censes at any time. And when any increase is made unless 
the same is paid in thirty days, the license shall be re- 
nor shall it abridge the right of the Jefferson County 


A-157 


Commission to require a license for any business, voca- 
tion, occupation, traffic, calling or profession not in- 
cluded in the above schedule. The Jefferson County 
Commission may decrease or revise any license retroac- 
tive to October 1, of any fiscal year, if in the opinion of 
said Commission, said schedule is unfair or unjust. 


Section 8: TERMS FOR WHICH LICENSE ISSUED: 
The amount of the license due hereunder by any one 
upon commencing business, shall not be affected or de- 


creased by the subsequent abandonment or discontinu- 
ance of such business. 


Section 9: LICENSE DEEMED A PERSONAL PRIVI- 
LEGE: (a) Every license shall be held to confer a per- 
sonal privilege to transact the business, employment or 
profession which may be the subject of the license, and 
shall not be exercised except by the person, firm, or cor- 
poration licensed, unless specifically authorized by law to 
do so. (b) A business or privilege for which such license 
is issued is, under actual sale, transferred to a new owner- 
ship in which case a transfer of license may be effected by 
application to the Director of Revenue and the payment 
of a fee of One Dollar ($1.00). No transfer shall be valid 
until same has been endorsed across the face of the li- 
cense receipt by the Director of Revenue. 


Section 10: SALARIED PROFESSIONAL EMPLOY. 
EES: When any person claims any concession on any 
kind of professional license provided for in this Code 
because of such person being either the employer or the 
employee, such person shall not be exempt from pay- 
ment of the license provided in this Code, until both tre 
employer and the employee have furnished to the Direc- 
tor of Revenue, sworn affidavits or other evidence satis- 
factory to the Director, setting out all the facts and condi- 
tions in regard to the employment with such facts and 


A-158 
conditions being as would ordinarily substantiate the em- 


ployment claim. 


Section 11: PLACE OF BUSINESS TO BE DESIG- 
NATED: Any person desiring to engage in any trade, 
business, vocation, occupation, profession, for which a 
license is required and for which a fixed place of business 
is required shall designate the place at which said trade, 
business, vocation, occupation or profession is to be car- 
ried on, and the license to be issued thereunder, shall 
designate such place and such license shall authorize the 
carrying on of such trade, business, vocation, or profes- 
sion only at such place. For each place at which said 
trade, business, vocation, occupation or profession is car- 
ried on, a separate license fee and tax shall be paid. 


Section 12: EACH BUSINESS DEMANDS SEPARATE 
LICENSE: Any person, firm or corporation dealing in 
two or more of the articles or engaging in two or more of 
the businesses, vocations, occupations, callings or profes- 
sions enumerated in Section 2 of this Code and for which 
a license is required of each, such person shall pay for 
and take out a license for each line of business, vocation, 
occupation, calling or profession, except as otherwise 
provided herein. 


Section 13: PAYMENT OF LICENSE BY CHECK: 
Whenever a license or license receipt shall be issued in 
return for any check, the same shall not be valid or of 
any force or effect unless such check shall be duly paid 
upon presentation to the drawee. 


Section 14: LICENSE TO BE EXHIBITED: Each li- 
cense shall be posted in a conspicuous place, where such 
business, vocation, occupation or profession is carried 
on, and the holder of such license shall immediately 
show such license to the Director of Revenue, or his 


A-159 


deputy, or any law enforcement officer of the County 


upon being so requested by such Director, deputy or of- 
ficer so to do. 


Section 15: All licenses under this Code shall be due 
and payable on the first day of October, 1989, for the 
County fiscal year, or from the day on which business is 
commenced if after October 1, 1989, for the remainder 
of the fiscal year and on October 1, for each subsequent 
County fiscal year. The payment for the fiscal year shall 
be delinquent on November 1, 1989 and each November 
1, thereafter for subsequent fiscal years. If said licenses 
are not paid before becoming delinquent a penalty shall 
be collected in an amount equal to 15% of the license 
due for such period, and also a citation fee of $1.50 for 
each license listed on the citation and served on a delin- 
quent. But the payment of any and all licenses due may 
be enforced immediately after the due date thereof. The 
Director of Revenue shall have the authority to waive 
delinquent penalty and interest for a good and sufficient 
cause shown. 


Section 16: No provision of this Code shall be applied: 
(a) so as to impose any unlawful tax or unlawful burden 
on interstate commerce or on activity of the State or 
Federal governments; or (b) in any manner which is re- 
pugnant to or violates any provision of the United States 
or Alabama constitutions. Nor shall the provisions of this 
Code apply to any business, vocation, occupation, calling 
or profession which has been specifically exempted un- 
der Act 406 as passed in the 1967 Regular Session of the 


Legislature and approved by the Governor on September 
7, 1967. 


Section 17: PROVISIONS SEPARABLE AS TO INDI- 
VIDUALITY, ETC.: Should any section or provision of 


A-160 


this Code be for any reason held void or invalid, it shall 
not affect the validity of any other section or provision 
hereof which is not itself void or invalid. 


Section 18: UNLAWFUL TO DO BUSINESS BEFORE 
OBTAINING LICENSE: It shall be unlawful for any per- 
son to engage in any business or vocation in the County 
without first having procured a license therefor, and 
each day during which said business or occupation is so 
engaged in shall constitute a separate offense. 


Section 19: (a) Any lawful license issued to any per- 
son, firm or corporation to conduct any business shall be 
subject to revocation by the Jefferson County Commis- 
sion for the violation by the licensee, his agent, servant, 
or employee, of any provision of this Code or of any 
ordinance of the County, or of any statute of the State of 
Alabama, relating to the business for which such license 
is issued: and shall also be subject to revocation by the 
Commission if the licensee, his agent, servant, or em- 
ployee under color of such license violates or aids or 
abets in violating or knowingly permits or suffers to be 
violated any penal ordinance of the County or any crimi- 
nal law of the State of Alabama; and shall also be subject 
to revocation by the Jefferson County Commission if, in 
connection with the issuance or renewal of any license, 
the licensee or his agent filed any application, affidavit, 
statement, or other document containing any untrue or 
misleading statement or omission of a material fact. 

(b) The conditions hereinabove set forth as grounds 
for the revocation of a license shall constitute grounds 
for the Jefferson County Commission refusing to renew a 
license. 

(c) The Commission shall set a time for hearing on 
the matter of revoking or refusing to renew a license; and 
a notice of such hearing shall be given to the licensee, or 


A-161 


the applicant for renewal, as the case may be, at least one 
day before the day set for said hearing. At the hearing 
the Jefferson County Commission shall hear all evidence 
offered by any party and all evidence that may be pre- 
sented bearing upon the question of revocation or the 
refusal of renewal, as the case may be. 


Section 20: FALSE OATH TO PROCURE LICENSE: 
It shall be unlawful for any person to give, submit, ex- 
hibit or present to the County, the Director of Revenue, 
or to any license inspector or collector of the County, any 
false, deceptive or misleading statement, affidavit, certifi- 
cate, book or record entry, or other document relating to 
the amount of capital invested, the value of goods, stocks, 
or merchandise, the amounts or volume of sales, receipts 
or commissions, the number of employees, the number 
of vehicles used, or any other data, record of information 
required by the License Code of the County to deter- 
mine the amount of license due. 


Section 21: Any person liable for any license hereun- 
der who neglects or refuses to pay the same, the amount 
thereof, including penalty, together with any cost which 
may accrue in addition thereto shall be a lien in favor of 
Jefferson County, Alabama, upon all property and the 
rights to property, real or personal, belonging to such 
person. Such lien shall not be valid against any mort- 
8agee, purchaser in the usual course of trade or conven- 
tional judgment creditor with an actual money judgment 
or a decree of some Court of Law or Equity until after 
the time a notice of such lien has been filed by the Direc- 
tor of Revenue in the Office of the Judge of Probate of 


the County in which such property, real or personal, is 
located. 


A-162 


Section 22: REFUNDS, ADDITIONAL LICENSES: (a) 
Any person who by mistake of fact or law has paid to the 
Director of Revenue money that was not due from him 
for a license or an amount in excess of that required by 
law for the business, vocation, occupation or profession 
to be carried on by such person under the license, shall 
be enutled to have refunded the money incorrectly paid, 
less the issuance fee and any commission which may he 
retained by the Director of Revenue. 

(b) Such application for refund must be made within 
two years from the date of such erroneous or excessive 
payment. 

(c) In case of the issuance of a license for less than 
the amount due therefor upon payment of the additional 
amount due for such license an additional license may be 
issued in such manner as to allow credit for the amount 
previously paid by the licensee and there shall be en- 
dorsed on the face of such additional license the words 
“additional to license # 


Section 23: DISPOSITION OF MONIES BY DIREC- 
TOR OF REVENUE: Within twenty days after the end of 
each month the Director of Revenue must remit to the 
County Treasurer all the money received by him for li- 
censes. 


Section 24: Any person violating any provision of this 
Code shall upon conviction be punished within the limits 
of and as provided by law. 


Section 25: This License Code of Jefferson County, 
Alabama, shall become effective as a law and ordinance 
of Jefferson County on October 1, 1989, following its 
adoption and approval by Ordinance of the Jefferson 
County Commission.

CODE OF ALABAMA 1975 


TITLE 11 
COUNTIES AND MUNICIPAL CORPORATIONS 


§ 11-51-90. Licensing of conduct of trade, business, 
profession, etc., in municipality authorized 
generally; licensing as to persons, etc., 
engaged in business in connection with 
interstate commerce; purposes for which 
licensing power conferred by division may 
be exercised. 

(a) Municipalities shall have the following powers: 

(1) To license any exhibition, trade, busi- 
ness, vocation, occupation, or profession not pro- 
hibited by the Constitution or laws of the state 
which may be engaged in or carried on in the city 
or town. 

(2) To fix the amount of licenses, the time 
for which they are to run, not exceeding one 
year, to provide a penalty for doing business with- 
out a license, and to charge a fee of not exceed- 
ing five dollars ($5) for issuing each license. 

(3) To require sworn statements as to the 
amount of capital invested, value of goods or 
stocks, or amounts of sales or receipts where the 
amount of the license is made to depend upon 
the amount of capital invested, value of goods or 
stocks, or amount of sales or receipts and to pun- 
ish any person or corporation for failure or re- 
fusal to furnish sworn statements or for giving of 
false statements in relation thereto. 


A-164 


(b) The license authorized by subsection (a) of this 
section as to persons, firms, or corporations engaged in 
business in connection with interstate commerce shall be 
confined to that portion within the limits of the state and 
where the person, firm, or corporation has an office or 
transacts business in the city or town imposing the li- 
cense. othe aly 

(c) The power to license conferred by this division 
may be used in the exercise of the police power as well as 
for the purpose of raising revenue, or both. (Code 1907, 
§ 1339; Acts 1909, No. 121, p. 121; Code 1923, § 2154; 
Code 1940, T. 37, § 735; Acts 1994, No. 94-644, § 1).

CITY OF BIRMINGHAM, ALABAMA 


ORDINANCE NO. 70-75 


AN ORDINANCE TO PROVIDE FOR AN OCCUPA- 
TIONAL LICENSE FEE FOR PERSONS ENGAGED IN 
TRADES, OCCUPATIONS AND PROFESSIONS, AND 


BE IT ORDAINED By the Council of the City of Birming- 
ham, Alabama, as follows: 


Section 1. DEFINITIONS. That the following words, 
when used in this ordinance, shall have the meaning as- 
cribed to them, except where the context clearly indi- 
cates or requires a different meaning: 

(a) The word “Person” shall mean any natural per- 
son. Whenever the word “Person” is used in any clause 
prescribing and imposing a penalty in the nature of a 
fine or imprisonment, the word as applied to a partner- 
ship or other form of unincorporated enterprise shall 
mean the partners or members thereof, and as applied to 
corporations shall mean the officers and directors 
thereof. 

(b) The words “Trade, Occupation and Profession” 
shall mean and include the doing of any kind of work, 
the rendering of any kind of personal services, or the 
holding of any kind of position or job within the City of 
Birmingham, Alabama, by any clerk, laborer, tradesman, 
manager, official or other employee, including any non- 
resident of the City of Birmingham who is employed by 
any employer as defined in this section, where the rela- 
tionship between the individual performing the services 
and the person for whom such services are rendered is, 
as to those services, the legal relationship of employer 


A-166 


and employee, including also a partner of a firm or an 
officer of a firm or corporation, if such partner or officer 
receives a salary for his personal services rendered in the 
business of such firm or corporation, but they shall not 
mean or include domestic servants employed in private 
homes or businesses, professions or occupations for 
which license fees are required to be paid under any 
General License Code of the City. 

(c) The words “Trade, Occupations and Professions” 
shall also mean and include the holding of any kind of 
office or position, either by election or appointment, by 
any federal, state, county or city Officer or employee 
where the services of such official or employee are ren- 
dered within the City of Birmingham. 

(d) The word “Employee” shall mean and include 
any person engaging in or following any trade, occupa- 
tion or profession within the meaning of subsection (b) 
of Section | of this ordinance. 

(e) The word “Employer” shall mean and include 
any person, business, firm, corporation, partnership, as- 
sociation or any other kind of organization who or that 
employs any person in any trade, occupation or profes- 
sion in the City of Birmingham within the meaning of 
subsection (b) of Section | of this ordinance. 

(f) The words “Gross Receipts” and “Compensation” 
shall have the same meaning, and both words shall mean 
and include but not be limited to the total of the follow- 
ing items which a person receives from or is entitled to 
receive from or be given credit for by his employer for 
any work done or personal services rendered in any 
trade, occupation or profession, including any kind of 
deductions before “Take Home” pay is received: 


Cash Compensation Non-Cash Compensation 
Salary Reimbursements for expenses 
Wages above actually incurred 


Bonuses Value of Personal Use of 


A-167 
Incentive Pay Company Owned Vehicle 
Overtime Pay Value of Personal Use of Clubs 
Commissions Cost of more than $50,000 group 


Severance Pay life insurance 


Non-Deminimus Awards in 
excess of $100.00 


The words “Gross Receipts” and “Compensation” shall 
also include the amount which an employee elects to 
defer pursuant to a salary reduction agreement under a 
retirement plan or deferral arrangement (including but 
not limited to 26 United States Code Sections 401 (k) or 
403(b) plans, but not Section 125 Cafeteria Plans). 

The words “Gross Receipts” and “Compensation” shall 
not include the following items: 


Employer Paid Insurance 
Educational Assistance 
Employee Discounts 
Stock Options 
Stock Grants Subject to a Substantial Risk of Forfei- 
ture under 26 United States Code Section 83 
Retirement Pay 
Distributions from Qualified 
Pension, Profit Sharing, Thrift, Stock Bonus, 
and Other Deferred Compensation Plans Quali- 
fied under 26 United States Code Section 
401 (a)

Non-Qualified Deferred 
Compensation Plans

Students Grant/Scholarships

Unemployment Compensation

tions to NonQualified Deferred Compensation 
Plans

(g) The word “Licensee” shall mean and include any 
person required to file a return or to pay a license fee 
under this ordinance. 

(h) The word “City” shall mean the City of Birming- 


ham, Alabama. 
(i) The words “Director of Finance” shall mean the 


Director of Finance of the City of Birmingham. 
(j) The singular shall include the plural and vice 
versa, and the masculine shall include the feminine and 


the neuter. 


Section 2. LICENSE FEES REQUIRED. It shall be un- 
lawful for any person to engage in or follow any trade, 
occupation or profession as defined in Section | within 
the City on and after the Ist day of December, 1970 with- 
out paying license fees for the privilege of engaging in or 
following such trade, occupation or profession, which li- 
cense fees shall be measured by one per centum (1%) of 
the gross receipts of each such person. 


Section 3. WHERE WORK DONE OR SERVICES 
PERFORMED BOTH WITHIN AND/WITHOUT THE 
CITY. In cases where compensation is earned as a result 
of work done or services performed both within and 


A-169 


without the city, the license fees required under this ordi- 
nance shall be computed by determining upon the oath 
of the employer, or if required by the Director of Fi- 
nance upon the oath of the employee, that percentage of 
the compensation earned from the proportion of the 
work which was done or performed within the City. 


Section 4. EMPLOYERS TO WITHHOLD LICENSE 
FEES AND FILE RETURNS. Each employer shall deduct 
from each payment due each employee the amount of 
a eee by one per centum (1%) of the 
pensation due each employee inning on the Ist 
day of December, 1970. The eae saanteed to be 
made on account of such deductions by employers shall 
be made monthly to the City for the monthly periods 
ending January 31, February 28, March 31, April 30, May 
31, June 30, July 31, August 31, September 30, October 
31, November 30, and December 31st of each year, on or 
before the twentieth day of the, month next following 
the end of each such monthly period, and each employer 
shall at the same time make a return in connection there- 
with on a form made available to such employer by the 
Director of Finance at the office of the Director of Fi- 
mr ae a total amount deducted from 
to or due all employees of an employer is 

less than fifty (50.00) dollars during each calendar 
month of the previous calendar year, then such employer 
may elect, for the current calendar year, to remit such 
deductions to the City for the quarterly periods ending 
March 3ist, June 30th, September 30th and December 
31st of such following calendar year, on or before the 
twentieth day of the month next following the end of 
each such quarterly period, and each such employer 
shail at the same time make a return in connection there- 
with on a form made available to such employer by the 
Director of Finance at the office of the Director of Fi- 


A-170 


nance. Provided, however, that the failure or omission by 
any employer to deduct such license fees shall not relieve 
an employee from the payment of such license fees and 
compliance with the requirements for making returns as 
provided in this Ordinance, or with any regulations pro- 
mulgated under this Ordinance. Each employer shall file 
in the office of the Director of Finance on or before 
January 31, 1971, a return on a form made available by 
said Director of Finance, at the office of said Director of 
Finance, which return shall show the gross amount of 
compensation of each employee, the amount of license 
fees deducted and paid by such employer for all or any 
part of the period from and including December 1, 1970, 
and the last known address of each such employee, and, 
on or before January 31st of each year thereafter a return 
showing the gross amount of compensation of each em- 
ployee and the amount of the license fees deducted and 
paid by the employer, during all or any part of the pre- 
ceding calendar year. Each employer shall keep accurate 
records of all such compensation, deductions, license 
fees, payments and returns. Such records shall be kept 
and maintained by each such employer for not less than 
(5) years subsequent to the date such compensation was 
earned. 


Section 5. RETURNS TO BE FILED BY EMPLOYEES. 
When a monthly or quarterly return, as required by Sec- 
tion 4 hereof is not filed by an employer and the license 
fees are not paid to the City by such employer monthly as 
herein provided, the employee for whom no return has 
been filed and no payment has been made shall file a 
return with the Director of Finance on or before the Ist 
day of the second month next following the end of each 
such monthly or quarterly period, showing in said return 
his gross receipts subject to license fees for such month 
or quarter, and he shall also file a return with the Direc- 


A-171 


tor of Finance on or before January 31st of each year 
thereafter in which his employer has failed to file any 
monthly or quarterly return required in the preceding 
calendar year, showing on said return the gross receipts 
subject to license fees during the preceding calendar 
year. If for any reason all license fees of a person subject 
to the provisions of this Ordinance were not withheld by 
his employer from his gross receipts, such person shall 
file each return required by this Section on a form ob- 
tainable at the office of the Director of Finance. In addi- 
tion to the gross receipts earned by him, such return 
shall show such other pertinent information as may be 
required by the Director of Finance. Each person making 
a return required by the Section shall, at the time of 
filing thereof, pay to the City the amount of license fees 
due under this Ordinance; provided, however, that any 
portion of the license fees deducted at the source shall 
be deducted on the return and only the balance, if any, 
shall be due and payable at the time of filing said return. 
Each employee shall keep accurate records of all such 
compensation, deductions, license fees, payments and re- 
turns. Such records shall be kept and maintained by each 
such employee for not less than five (5) years subsequent 
to the date such compensation was earned. 


prescribe printed forms for use by persons subject to the 
provisions of this ordinance and shall make such forms 
available at his office for use by such person. 


A-172 


Section 7. INVESTIGATIVE POWERS OF THE DIREC- 
TOR OF FINANCE. The Director of Finance or any 


agent or employee designated by him is hereby autho- 
rized to examine the books, papers and records of any 


employer or supposed employer, or of any licensee or 
supposed licensee, in order to determine the accuracy of 
any return made, or if no return was made to ascertain 
the amount of license fees due under the terms of this 
ordinance by such examination. Each such employer or 
supposed employer or licensee or supposed licensee shall 
give to the Director of Finance, or to his duly authorized 
agent or employee, the means, facilities and opportunity 
for the making of such examination and investigation. 
The Director of Finance is hereby authorized to examine 
any person under oath concerning any gross receipts 
which were or should have been shown in a return, and 
to this end he may compel the production of books, pa- 
pers, records and the attendance of all persons before 
him, whether as parties or as witnesses, whom he believes 
to have knowledge of such gross receipts or compensa- 


non. 


Section 8. REGULATIONS MAY BE PROMULGATED. 
The Director of Finance may prescribe, adopt, promul- 
gate and enforce reasonable rules and regulations not in 
conflict with this ordinance relating to any matter or 
thing pertaining to the administration and enforcement 
of the provisions of this ordinance, including but not 
limited to provisions for the re-examination and correc- 
tion of returns as to which overpayment or underpay- 
ment is claimed or found to have been made, and the 
regulations so promulgated shall be binding upon all lic- 
ensees and employers. 


Section 9. INFORMATION TO BE CONFIDENTIAL. 
Notwithstanding any ordinances to the contrary, any in- 


A-173 


formation gained by the Director of Finance or any other 
official or agent or employee of the City as a result of any 
returns, investigations, hearings or verifications required 
or authorized by this ordinance shall be confidential, ex- 
cept for official purposes, or in accordance with proper 
judicial order, or the enforcement of this ordinance, and 
any person or agent divulging such information, except 
as herein permitted, shall upon conviction be subject to a 
fine of not more that $100 or to imprisonment of not 
exceeding thirty (30) days, or to both such fine and im- 
prisonment. 


Section 10. INTEREST AND PENALTIES 

(a) All license fees imposed by this ordinance which 
are delinquent, that is, which remain unpaid after they 
become due shall, subject to the provisions herein- 
after set out, bear interest at the rate of twelve per- 
centum (12%) per annum, and any person who has 
failed to pay such license fees when the same became 
due shall, subject to the provisions hereinafter set out. 
also be charged a penalty of ten percentum (10%) of the 
amount of such unpaid license fees. Any person or em- 
ployer who fails or refuses to withhold any license fees 
payable under this ordinance, or who fails to pay such 
fees after withholding the same to the City at the time it 
is due, as provided under the provisions of Section 4 
hereof, shall become liable to the City for such fees, as 
well as for the interest thereon at the rate of twelve 
percentum (12 %) per annum, and for the aforesaid 
penalty. Provided, however, that the minimum penalty 
imposed against such person or employer shall be Three 
Dollars ($3.00). Provided the Director of Finance, if a 
good and sufficient reason is shown for the failure to pay 
the tax within the time required, may waive or remit the 
penalty and interest, or a portion of either, upon pay- 
ment of the tax due. 


A-174 


(b) Any person or employee who shall fail, neglect or 
refuse to pay a license fee as by this ordinance required, 
or any employer who shall fail to withhold said license 
fees, or to pay over to City such license fees, penalties or 
interest imposed by this ordinance, or any person re- 
quired to file a return under the provisions of Section 5 
of this ordinance, who shall fail, neglect or refuse to file 
such return, or any person or employer who shall refuse 
to permit the Director of Finance or any agent or em- 
ployee designated by him, in writing, to examine his 
books, records and papers for any purpose authorized by 
this ordinance, or who shall knowingly make any incom- 
plete, false or fraudulent return, or who shall attempt to 
do anything whatever to avoid the full disclosure of the 
amount of gross receipts or compensation in order to 
avoid the payment of the whole or any part of a license 
fee, shall upon conviction be subject to punishment 
within the limits of and as provided by Section 1-6 of the 
General Code of the City of Birmingham, 1964, for each 
offense. Such punishment shall be in addition to the 
penalties imposed under subsection (a) of this section. 
(Subsection (b) of Section 10 amended by Ord. No. 73- 
77 adopted June 12,1973.) 


Section 11. EXTENSION OF TIME FOR MAKING RE- 
TURN. The Director of Finance, for good cause, may 
extend the time for making any return required under 
the provisions of this ordinance, but the time for filing 
any such return shall not be extended for a greater pe- 
riod than thirty (30) days from the day such return is due 
to be made and shall not prevent penalty and interest 
from accruing during the period of such extension. 


Section 12. USE OF LICENSE FEES. All money derived 
from license fees under the provisions of this ordinance 
shall be paid to City and placed to the credit of the Gen- 


A-175 


eral Fund of the City of Birmingham, and shall be used 
and expended as authorized by law. 


Section 13. SEVERABILITY. The provisions of this ordi- 
nance are severable. If any provision, section, paragraph, 
sentence or part thereof, or the application thereof to 
any employer or licensee or class or persons, shall be 
held unconstitutional or invalid, such decision shall not 
affect or impair the remainder of the ordinance, it being 
the legislative intent to ordain and enact each provision, 


section, paragraph, sentence and part thereof, separately 
and independently of each other. 


Section 14. REPEAL OF ORDINANCES. All ordinances 


or parts of ordinances in conflict with this ordinance are 
to the extent of such conflict hereby repealed. 


Section 15. EFFECTIVE DATE. This ordinance shall be 
in full force and effect on and after December 1, 1970, 
and from year to year thereafter, until repealed.

IN THE 
SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES 
OCTOBER TERM 1995 


JASON RICHARDS and FANNIE HILL, 
Petits ' 
v. 


JEFFERSON COUNTY, ALABAMA 
and BIRMINGHAM-JEFFERSON CIVIC 
CENTER AUTHORITY, 


Respondents. 
DECLARATION OF RANDY GODEKE 


(i) My name is Randy Godeke, and I am over 21 
years of age. 

(ii) Ihave been employed as Director of Revenue by 
Respondent, Jefferson County, Alabama, since June 29, 
1991. By virtue of my official position, | have personal 
knowledge of the facts declared herein, and I am autho- 
rized to make this declaration. 

(iii) As Director of Revenue for Jefferson County, | 
am responsible for the collection of all tax revenues, 
other than property taxes, of Jefferson County, including 
business license and occupational taxes. 

(iv) An occupational tax (the “occupational tax”) is 
levied by Jefferson County pursuant to Ordinance No. 
1120, adopted on September 29, 1987. 

(v) Section 4 of Ordinance No. 1120 requires each 
employer whose employees are subject to the occupa- 


A-186 


tional tax to “file in the office of the Director of Revenue 
on or before January 31, of each year a return... which 
return shall show the gross amount of compensation of 
each employee, the amount of license fees deducted and 
paid by such employer for all or any part of the preced- 
ing calendar year. . . .” 

(vi) Section 6 of Ordinance No. 1120 states that the 
Director of Revenue shall “keep records showing the 
amounts received by him from each employer.” 

(vii) Pursuant to Section 9 of Ordinance No. 1120, 
subject to certain exceptions, information regarding indi- 
vidual employees is required to be kept confidential. 
However, in the regular course of business, the Depart- 
ment of Revenue develops selective statistics for certain 
representative employers, including the total number of 
employees, the gross wages paid by the employer, the 
amount of wages upon which the occupational tax was 
withheld, the percentage of gross wages subject to the 
occupational tax, and, where applicable, the reason or 
reasons that certain employees of the employer were not 
subject to the occupational tax. Such statistics are main- 
tained by my office in the regular course of business and 
are public records; they are available upon request for 
inspection by the public. 

(viii) Attached hereto as Exhibit A to this Declaration 
is a compilation of statistics for the 1994 calendar year 
pertaining to representative employers in various catego- 
ries of businesses, industries and professions. These statis- 
tics are based on the returns submitted by such employ- 
ers for the 1994 calendar year, which is the latest year for 
which such statistics have been developed and compiled 
by my office. The compilation—based on the legally re- 
quired returns filed by employers, as translated to public 
records—is incorporated in, and adopted as a part of, 
this Declaration. 


A-187 


(ix) The compilation reflected by Exhibit A presents 
various categories of businesses, industries and profes- 
sions and representative companies or firms within each 
such category, identifying for each such company or firm 
the total number of W-2 tax returns filed for employees 
of such company or firm during 1994, the gross wages 
paid by such employer in that year, the amount of wages 
that were subject to the occupational tax, the percentage 
of the gross wages subject to the occupational tax, and a 
code explaining, where applicable, the reasons that the 
occupational tax applied to less than 100% of the gross 


wages paid by such company or firm. 
(x) This Declaration is made under penalty of per- 


jury. 
/8/ Randy Godeke 
RANDY GODEKE 
Director of Revenue

Despite provisions of the Constitution of 1901 to pro- 
vide educational financing through the Public School 
Fund to meet the constitutional mandate to “establish, 
organize and maintain a liberal system of public schools 
throughout the State for the benefit of all children 
thereof between the ages of seven and twenty-one years,” 
it was obvious that the Public School Fund was inad- 
equate (Constitution of 1901, Section, 256). The follow- 
ing revenues accrued to the Public School Fund: 


A-196 


Therefore, only the statewide 3.0 mill property tax actu- 
ally provided significant revenue to the Public School 
Fund. 

At the local level, constitutional taxes consisted of the 
1.0 mill and 3.0 mill taxes to be levied at the county level. 
However, these were not mandatory unless approved by 
the local taxpayers. A district property tax of 3.0 mills 
could also be levied with the consent of the voters and 
only if the 3.0 mill county tax was levied first. Therefore, 
the only significant funding at the local level was also 
based on the property tax. 

Many different funds had been established since 1901, 
with the majority of these receiving revenues through the 
General Fund. And the major source of revenue for the 
General Fund was again the property tax. Such heavy 
reliance upon the property tax, coupled with the state's 
efforts to create a statewide system of elementary and 
secondary schools with a minimum seven-month term, 
placed undue stress on the property tax. While the 
growth in property tax was certain, it was slow and did 
not have the growth potential to meet all needs of educa- 
tion. Therefore, the 1927 legislature was forced to look 
elsewhere to meet rising needs of both elementary-sec- 
ondary education and higher education. 


The Seven-Month School Term, 1927 

A major campaign issue in the gubernatorial election 
of 1927 had been the problem of providing a uniform 
seven-month school term for all children in Alabama. 
There had appeared no argument against the seven- 
month school term, only disagreement regarding the 
means of financing it. This problem was fully docu- 
mented in the preceding chapter and will not be dis 
cussed here. In order to accomplish the seven-month 
school term, two factors had to be addressed: one was the 
inadequate revenue available to education; the second 


A-197 


was the problem of how to provide funds for the poorer 
counties so as to make the money available to schools 
more nearly equal throughout the state. Previously 
(1919), the Revolving Fund of $100,000 annually had 
been made available to promote equalization; this 


amount was grossly inadequate: 


The Joint Committee on Education of the 
Legislature of 1923 unanimously recommended 
increasing this fund to a million dollars. More 
recent studies show that in order to carry out an 
equalization program an even larger sum would 
be necessary. It would require not less than one 
and one-half or two million dollars. This 
amount would scarcely give a term of seven 
months; and to do this, even on the present low 
salary schedule, it would be necessary for all fees 
now collected, amounting in the aggregate to a 
million or more dollars annually, to be contin- 
ued as a part of the school fund. If this private 
source of income is to be eliminated, it will be 
necessary to provide an amount totaling two 
and one-half to three million dollars in order to 
enable the schools in every county to remain in 
session for a period of one hundred and forty 
days on the present cost basis, which is far below 


re en average (Annual Report, 1926, pp. 
). 


The effort to provide the seven-month school term would 
involve equalizing financial opportunity for 219 public 
school units in the state, 67 county systems, 63 county 
high schools, 11 state secondary agricultural schools, 50 
city systems, and 28 town systems (Annual Report, 1926, p. 
80). As the state began its efforts in 1927 to promote 
equalization, and in effect, a minimum foundation pro- 
gram, the problem of inadequate financial support for 
this measure was evident. This problem would recur later 
in 1935 with the Minimum Program Act. 


A-198 


In order to understand the conditions which contrib- 
uted to shaping the financial situation of Alabama's 
schools in 1927, it is advisable to look back a few years. As 
Alabama was the only state in the union in which the 
legislature met in regular session only once in four years, 
the history of state support of public education in Ala- 
bama naturally divides itself into four-year periods. 

Before 1927, the state did not attempt to equalize edu- 
cational opportunity on a comprehensive basis. The first 
public school law, enacted in 1854, recognized the then 
existing inequalities of opportunity and endeavored to 
overcome them by providing that townships receiving in- 
come from sixteenth section lands granted by the federal 
government should not share in the legislative appropria- 
tions for education until townships receiving no such in- 
come were brought up to parity with those enjoying in- 
come from public land. 

By provisions of the Constitution of 1901, the only 
substantial state support provided the public schools 
through the preceding quadrennia to 1927 was the Pub- 
lic School Fund, which was apportioned on a school 
census basis. The Public School Fund, comprised of 
the three-mill constitutional tax for schools, interest on 
sixteenth section funds, certain other minor sources of 
revenue, and an appropriation of $500,000 from the 
state General Fund, amounted to only about $3,515,000 
annually during the 1923 quadrennium. Certain other 
special appropriations amounting to approximately 
$1,017,000 annually were made for county high schools, 
state secondary agricultural schools, etc., but these 
funds were not available for general distribution. During 
the quadrennium ending in 1927, five-month elemen- 
tary terms and six-month high school terms were 
common in the white schools of about seventeen coun- 
ties of the state (Annual Report, 1935-37, Part 1, p. 14). 
The issue was clear that the school term must be in- 


A-199 


creased and that new sources of revenue would have to 
be found. 


Heat name Special Rducatonal Trust Pund Enacted, 


In the message of newly elected Governor Graves to 
the legislature on June 7, 1927, he defined the main busi- 
ness before the body as that of finding the revenue re- 
quired for the needs of the schools. He said that “Ala- 
bama can afford all the millions of dollars that her neces- 
sities demand, but she cannot afford one penny that is 
not necessary” (Graves, June 7, 1927, p. LXXXVI, Regu- 
lar Session). 

In searching for additional revenue sources, the Recess 
Committee on Ways and Means recognized that school 
revenue came from only two sources: taxes on ad valo- 
rem property and legislative appropriations. Under the 
provisions of the constitution, the statewide 3.0 mill tax, 
the 1.0 mill county ad valorem tax, and the 3.0 mill 
county and 3.0 mill district ad valorem tax approved by 
constitutional amendment in 1916 represented the mills 
of taxation already being collected on ad valorem prop- 
erty for school purposes. In addition, the constitutional 
poll tax collected and kept in the counties for school 
purposes did not yield much revenue. Thus, in 1927 
property owners were bearing the heaviest taxation in 
support of the state's school system. The legislature, rec- 
ognizing this, concluded that new sources other than ad 
valorem property taxes must be tapped (Cain, pp. 72-74). 

The Recess Committee on Ways and Means, having 
studied various methods of obtaining new revenue, re- 
ported a tax measure known as the Tunstall Revenue Bill 
to the House of Representatives. This bill proposed sev- 
eral kinds of luxury and nuisance taxes, especially in- 
creased levies on tobacco products. With Governor 


A-200 


Graves’ endorsement it passed the House on June 17, 
1927, by an overwhelming vote. 

The Tunstall Revenue Bill, however, did not place any 
additional taxation on railroads, iron and coal interests, 
and other large corporations. Therefore, it was very un- 
popular in many quarters, and some 50 prominent busi- 
nessmen appeared in Montgomery to voice their disap- 
proval. The Mobile Register assailed the House of Repre- 
sentatives as a lobbyist interest for the railroads, the 
power companies, and the mining companies (The Mo 
bile Register, June 18, 1927). 

The Graves strategy, unknown at this time, was to wait 
for a more favorable opportunity to kill the measure 
rather than attacking it in the House. The Senate Com- 
mittee on Finance and Taxation held public hearings on 
the Tunstall Bill, and on July 13, 1927, the measure was 
defeated by the Graves interests in the Senate. A modi- 
fied measure was enacted placing taxes on coal, iron ore, 
minerals, railroads, telephone, telegraph and express 
companies, hydroelectric power, and numerous other 
commodities. With the bill virtually rewritten and passed 
by a vote of 21 to four in the Senate, the House had little 
choice but to accept the Senate’s new version (Gilbert, p. 
32). 

Act No. 163 (H. 359—Tunstall) entitled “An Act In 
Reference to and to Further Provide for the General Rev- 
enue of the State of Alabama” was approved by Governor 
Graves on July 22, 1927. Section 2 of the Act specified 
new revenue sources to be levied for educational pur- 


poses: 

2-A. A tax of 2 % per cent on gross receipts of intrastate 
business of railroads. 

2-B. A tax of 2 % per cent on gross receipts of telegraph 
companies. 


A-201 


2C. A tax of 2 % per cent on gross receipts above 
$50,000 of telephone companies. 


2-D. A tax of 2 % per cent on gross receipts of express 
companies. 

2-E. A tax of 2/5 of a mill on each kilowatt hour on 
hydroelectric companies. 


2-F. A tonnage tax of 2 4% cents per ton on the mining 
of coal. 


24G. A tonnage tax of 4 4% cents per ton on the mining 
of iron ore. 


2-H. A severance tax on sand, gravel, slag and other min- 
eral products other than coal and iron of 3 per 
cent on net value at mine after deducting cost of 
mining and quarrying. 

2-1. A tax of $20,000 per year on sleeping car compa- 
nies. 


2-J. A tax of 15 per cent on the wholesale price of ci- 
gars, cigarettes, and cheroots. 


Section 2-K specified the purpose of these revenues. 


2-K. All revenue collected under provisions of Section 
Two (2), 2-A, 2-B, 2-C, 2-D, 2-E, 2-F, 2-G, 2-H, 2-1, 
and 2-] of this Act shall be paid to the State Tax 
Commission of the State of Alabama by check or 
draft made payable to the State Treasurer and shall 
be set apart as a Trust Fund for Educational pur- 
poses only, to be designated as the Alabama Special 
Educational Trust Fund, and shall be kept separate 
and apart from all other funds in the State Trea- 
sury, and shall be paid out by the Treasurer on 
lawful appropriations, hereafter made specially 
from such funds by the Legislature of Alabama for 
educational purposes. The provisions of this sec- 
tion shall go into effect on October 1, 1927. (Acts 
of Alabama, 1927, No. 163, pp. 139-148). 


A-202 


The earmarking of these revenues to the credit of the 
Alabama Special Educational Trust Fund (ASETF), after 
deduction of the cost of collection, has been carried for- 
ward in the present code (Code of Alabama 1975, Sec- 
tion 40-1-31). Other taxes earmarked in the future to the 
credit of the ASETF carry other code citations. These will 
be discussed fully in Chapter VI. 

In addition, this Act placed privilege and license taxes 
upon insurance companies, accountants, insurance ad- 
justers, architects, attorneys, retail sales from railway cars, 
dentists, doctors, engineers, motor trucks, auto dealers, 
and assorted other professional and business pursuits, in- 
cluding a tax of two cents per gallon on lubricating oil. 
The total revenue from this bill was estimated to yield 
about $4 million in its first year. Practically all of this 
revenue would be generated from those taxes earmarked 
to the Alabama Special Educational Trust Fund (Acts of 
Alabama, 1927;>No. 163, pp. 139-187). 

With the passage of this revenue bill, Governor Graves 
commented: 


This is Alabama’s great day, her indepen- 
dence day. Today she is free and I thank God 
for this legislature that has done more for the 
rehabilitation of the state than any other in her 
history. (Montgomery Advertiser, July 15, 1927.) 


Unified Educational Program, 1927 


The legislative session of 1927 witnessed a well- 
intentioned endeavor to promote by generous appropria- 
tions the development of education in Alabama through 
Act 163, and through the Unified Educational Program 
Act, No. 382. The legislation of that year (embodied for 
the most part in the act of August 25, 1927, No. 382), in 
spite of its failure to provide adequate revenue to meet 
the additional expenditures authorized in the law, must 
be viewed as a significant forward step in the educational 


A-203 


history of the state. While financially incomplete or pre- 
mature, its purposes and provisions, considered from the 
standpoint of educational need, were basically sound. It 
is doubtful, however, whether it can be rightly termed a 
“Unified Educational Program” because it was accompa- 
nied by none of the changes in organization and it em- 
bodied none of the budgetary principles which were es- 
sential to the establishment and execution of such a pro- 
gram. The act contained the following appropriations: 


$900,000 to the Equalization Fund 

$850,000 to the Attendance Fund 

$18,300 to the Library Fund 

$128,500 to the Rural Schoolhouse Building Fund 
$37,500 to the Illiteracy Fund 

$200,706 for the Vocational Education Fund 
$411,000 to the County High School Fur" 
$32,000 to the State Department of Education 


The concluding section of the law provided that these 
appropriations should be payable out of the special trust 
fund created and set apart by Section 2 of the General 
Revenue Bill of 1927: “and in the event such trust fund is 
insufficient to cover said appropriations, then the bal- 
ance of the same shall be payable out of any funds in the 
treasury not otherwise appropriated.” This provision and 
its bearing on the general financial situation were signifi- 
cant in years to come (Brookings, 1932, pp. 282-283). 


SNEVeenw- 


Results of the Seven-Month School Term, 1927 


The legislature of 1927 not only provided additional 
funds for the support of public education, but set up the 
specific provisions for financing a program of education 
of not less than seven months. Definite objectives were 
sought in the expenditure of new funds, but the general 
dominating purpose was to provide an equal educational 
opportunity for all the children of the state. 


A-204 


Studies conducted in 1925 and 1926 led to the selec- 
tion of a $750 teacher unit for elementary grades and a 
$1,400 teacher unit for high schools. A teacher unit, as 
set out in the law, represented the total cost of education 
of thirty pupils in average daily attendance during any 
given school year of not less than seven months. Reduced 
to the lowest denomination, this meant $25.00 for the 
education of each elementary pupil and $46.66 for the 
education of each high school pupil. These figures were 
arrived at by finding the average cost per teacher or pu- 
pil unit in the state. The figures given above approximate 
what was found to be the average cost of education. The 
total of the new appropriations, it was thought, would 
provide sufficient funds to hold to these teacher unit 
values during the quadrennium. As a matter of fact, dur- 
ing the 1927-28 school year, not only was the amount 
available sufficient to do this, but an additional allowance 
of 3.5 percent of the total cost of the program was allo- 
cated to the counties eligible to share in the apportion- 
ment of the equalization fund. 

However, the increase in attendance was unprec- 
edented during 1927-28 and 1928-29 and went far be- 
yond the estimates on the basis of which the appropria- 
tions were made. There was an increase in aggregate at- 
tendance of more than 11 million school days over the 
two-year period. This naturally involved an increase in 
the number of teacher or pupil units. The distribution of 
the Equalization Fund to the counties had to be made on 
the basis of this increased attendance. Unfortunately, 
there was exactly the same amount of money available for 
distribution the following year, but the larger number of 
teacher units made the amount for each unit a little less. 
As a consequence, instead of having a surplus to distrib- 
ute as had been the case in the previous year, it became 
necessary to reduce the value of the teacher or pupil unit 
by 5.5 percent. This reduction made it necessary to lower 


A-205 


the cost of the equalization program from the teacher 
unit cost for the previous years ($750 and $1,400 teacher 
unit values) by about $350,000, which, in reality, was the 
amount by which the Equalization Fund should have 
been increased (Annual Report, 1929, pp. 21-22). 


The Great Depression, 1929 


Soon after the Unified Education Program enacted by 
the 1927 legislature and funded through the ASETF went 
into effect, reductions in school revenue rather than in- 
creases were the order of the day. School revenue 
reached its highest point during the 1928-29 school year. 
The decrease in revenue felt for the first time since the 
Constitution of 1901 was adopted came in the 1929-30 
school year. While the decrease was not serious, it her- 
alded the impending disaster of the 1931 quadrennium. 

Public expenditures for all educational purposes did 
not peak until 1929, while the percentage expenditure of 
educational funds for elementary and high schools de- 
creased steadily during the quadrennium (Brookings, p. 
262). As the economic situation grew worse, the problem 
of over-reliance on the property tax for both state and 
local education funding grew more acute. Whereas in 
1910 the state could collect 55.6 per cent of its total rev- 
enue from the property tax, by 1930 it could collect only 
26.6 per cent from this source. 

By 1930 the financial conditions of both the state and 
public education were becoming alarming. State Super- 
intendent A.F. Harmon summed up his fears for 
education’s future: 


That sacrifices and adjustments of one kind 
or another may have to be made seems inevi- 
table. But the distress of the State in years to 
come will be infinitely more pressing and the 
shame will be ours if we balance the budget with 


the ignorance of our children (Annual Report, 
1931, p. 43). 


A-206 


Public education had advanced greatly in Alabama 
during the 1927-31 quadrennium largely as the result of 
increased state support. White rural elementary schools 
generally operated a minimum term of seven months, 
white rural high schools operated nine months, and in 
the cities both elementary and high schools for whites 
generally operated for nine-month terms. The appear- 
ance was that educational conditions should continue to 
improve. 

But sad days were rapidly approaching for the 
public schools of Alabama. The effects of the 
depression were becoming apparent on every 
hand. The assessed valuation of property was 
being rapidly lowered and state revenues were 
tobogganing downward (Annual Report, 1935-37, 

p. 15). 


By 1929, educational expenditures had peaked on the 
basis of the educational program financed in the 1927 
quadrennium. During this period, the percentage of 
state funding for public education increased dramatically 
as an expanded school term and equalization were 
sought. This is shown in Table 41. 


Table 4-1

FY 1927 to FY 1931 

~ Total Stateand ‘Total State Percentage 
Year Local Funds Funds State Funds

A-207 


Whereas in 1927 the state had contributed less than 
one third of all funds derived from state and local rev- 
enues raised for public school purposes, by 1928 and 
1929 it contributed a little more than one third, and by 
1930 and 1931 nearly reached two fifths. 

This increasing percentage of state support for educa- 
tion would become a major problem in the next 
quadrennium. With state revenues being hardest hit by 
the depression, the proportionate shortfall that educa- 
tion could expect would be dramatically heightened as 
education became a “nonessential” function of state gov- 
ernment. However, this future was unforeseen as well as 
the devastating effects of the depression: 


When money is lacking for the retirement of 
debts contracted in good faith for educational 
purposes; when those who earn their living by 
furnishing the educational service demanded by 
the State must go unpaid, when the State, on 
account of lack of funds, must waive even tem- 
porarily efforts to provide instruction in amount 
and kind adequate to the needs of its young, 
there may not be occasion for alarm but there is 
need for the noblest kind of thinking on the 
part of all who are in any way concerned with 
education or are in any measure responsible for 
its effective functioning. These conditions are 
outcomes of economic depression (Annual Re- 
port, 1931, p. 28). 

In an argument of contemporary interest, a 
plea was made for not reducing any state fund- 
ing for public education: Admitting as we do 
the financial embarrassment of the State, it 
would be a tragic error to force the support of 
the public school system back upon the local 
units. Even in times of economic depression, 
such as we now suffer, it would be a cruel wrong 
to pay our public debt with money which should 
provide the children of this generation with 


A-208 


education that will enable them better to meet 
the obligations that fall due in the years to 
come. It is unthinkable that Alabama as a State 
will ever balance its budget at the expense of 
the cost of the public school system (Annual Re- 
port, 1931, pp. 30-31). 


This assumption was short-lived in the face of the rap- 
idly changing financial conditions of the state. Alabama 
did balance its budget at the expense of its children in 
the public schools.

But by 1930 the effects of the depression were influ- 
encing the appropriations promised education some 
four years earlier, and the school program suffered. Rev- 
enues were insufficient to meet school appropriations in 
1930, and as a result warrants were used to pay teachers 
salaries. The hardships that were to befall Alabama were 
in many ways similar to those of the Reconstruction Era 
(Cain, pp. 206-207). 

The governorship was assumed by B. M. Miller in 1931. 
Miller spoke optimistically of the state of education in his 
address tothe legislature, January 13, 1931: 


In education we have progressed all along the 
line. At the beginning of the last Session a 
$600,000 appropriation was made to bring the 
terms of rural schools up to a minimum of 
seven months and since that time, as a rule, this 
minitnum has been maintained. 

During the six years from 1924 to 1930, the 

e school term for all children in the pub- 
lic schools of Alabama increased 17 days, from 
133 days to 150 days. The term for elementary 
grades increased from 130 days to 145 days, or 


A-210 


15 days during this period, while the high 
school increased 5 days, from 165 to 170. 

The natural growth in the number, the exten- 
sion of terms, the greater attendance, and the 
expenses of consolidation have all increased to 
the point where our present appropriations are 
far short of our necessities. Alabama will not be 
true to herself and her own if she does not meet 
these needs and maintain this progress (Miller, 
Jan. 13, 193 1, p. VI-VII). 

However, Governor Miller's administration 
seemed unable to cope with the growing crisis 
precipitated by the Great Depression of 1929, 
and, by the 1932-33 school year, the picture had 
darkened considerably: 

The impending disaster broke in full force in 
the 1931-35 quadrennium. The people of the 
State were panic stricken. Educational appro- 
priations could not be paid due to a deficit in 
the State Treasury. Local school boards cut 
teachers’ salaries and instituted every possible 
economy but such measures could not possibly 
meet the deficit in school funds. Efforts were 

made to increase state revenues in order that 
education appropriations might be paid but 
those efforts failed. (Annual Report, 1935-37, p. 
15) 


This disaster was fully reflected in the educational condi- 
tions under which the school year 1932-33 operated. 


(a) over 50,000 white children or approxi- 
mately 21 percent of the children in the white 
rural schools of Alabama had school terms of 
three months and less. 


(b) over 129,000 white children or approxi- 
mately 55 percent of the children n the white 
rural schools had school terms of five and one- 
half months and less. 


A-211 


(c) over 48,000 regular students did not even 
enroll in school, either due to short terms or no 


available school openin (Annual Report 
37, p. 16). split vo rr 


Store License Tax, 1931 


The only additional tax passed for education during 
this period was the store license tax, approved in 1931, 
but it provided too little. The disaster would have to un- 
fold completely before major new revenues would be 
found for education. 

The store license tax, known as a “chain store tax” was 
passed by the legislature on July 7, 1931, to go into effect 
on October 1, 1931. The Act required a license to oper- 
ate an “establishment of stores” in this state. For one 
store license it imposed a $1 fee; for the next four stores 
the fee was $10 each; for the next five stores it was $15 
each; for the next ten stores it was $25 each: and for 
additional stores over twenty it was $75 each. In addition 
to the tax imposed, an application fee of 50 cents was 
required for each store. This tax ran with the calendar 
year, with half-year tax due after July | (Acts of Alabama 
1931, No. 369, p. 431). | 

The store license tax brought about considerable liti- 
gation. Although its constitutionality was attacked by the 
large chain store owners, the law did become operative 


a - 1, 1931, and was upheld in court in the fall of 


This tax was earmarked by Section 10. 


All money collected under the provisions of 
this Act, less the expenses incurred in the ad- 
ministration of this Act, shall be paid into the 
State Treasury, monthly, by the State Tax Com- 
se we oqo added to and shall consti- 
ute a part e Education Trust Fund (Acts 
Alabama, 1931, No. 369, p. 431). ee

The Brookings Institute Report, 1932 

With bankruptcy facing the state, the legislature passed 
an act on January 30, 1931, requesting the services of the 
Institute for Government Research of the Brookings In- 
stitute of Washington, D.C., to survey the state govern- 
ment. The areas studied would be the administrative 
structure of the state government, the state's existing fi- 
nancial condition including a review of financial opera- 
tions during recent years, and a review of the financial 
administration of the state and its political subdivisions, 
the taxation system of the state and the counties, and 
county government. The report was submitted to Gover- 
nor Miller on June 11, 1932 (Brookings Institute, p. 3). 


Financial Condition of the State, 1931. The report 
showed the state to be running a consolidated current 
deficit of $18,206,258.17 as of September 30, 1931. For 
the five-year period from 1927 to 1931 inclusive, the Gen- 
eral Fund receipts exceeded ordinary expenditures by 
$2,921,872.82. Mandatory transfers from the General 
Fund to cover other appropriated expenses, however, to- 
talled $15,713,526.75, leaving a deficit of $12,791 ,653.93. 
For 1931, the following transfers from the General Fund
