At this same time, the following local taxes were being 
used to support public education in addition to state rev- 


enues: 


1. The net amount of poll tax collected in the county 
(Constitution of 1901, Section 259). 

2. One-mill and three-mill county taxes, (Constitution 
of 1901, Section 269 and Amendment III, Article 
XIX, Section 1) neither of which is made mandatory 
by the constitution. 

3. Three-mill district tax allowable only if the county is 
levying not less than a 3.0 mill special county school 
tax (Constitution of 1901, Amendment III). 


It should be particularly noted that neither the 1.0 mill 
tax nor the 3.0 mill tax was made mandatory by the con- 
stitution on any county; no tax up to 4.0 mills could be 
levied by a county unless “the rate of such tax, the time it 
is to continue, and the purpose thereof, shall have been 
first submitted to a vote of the qualified electors of the 
county,” and voted for, in the case of the three-mill tax, 
by a majority of those voting. A further constitutional 
limitation on county school taxation provided that the 
total rate of taxation in any county for state and county 
combined should not exceed $1.25 on each $100 of tax- 
able property, which left only five mills to the county 
unearmarked (As a result of Amendment 3 the real cap 


was $1.85.). 


SNSEVeSwe> 


A-217 


Actual Levy of County Taxes, 1931. It appears that in 
1931 at least 56 counties were levying the 1.0 mill tax and 


all of the counties were levying the 3.0 mill tax. In addi- 
tion, four counties were levying additional countywide 
taxes for school purposes, with the result that in Baldwin 
Mobile, and Montgomery Counties, the total for school 
purposes amounted to 6.0 mills. Jefferson County was 
levying 4.5 mills. 

In 1931, there were 1,23 8 special school taxing dis- 
tricts in the state, exclusive of the municipalities, ranging 
from one in Lowndes County to 64 in Marshall and in 
Morgan Counties. The average number of special school 
taxing districts in each county was 18.4. Nearly all of 
these districts were levying the 3.0 mill tax, thus raising 
the total school tax rate to 7.0 mills. In only 13 districts 
was the tax rate 2.0 mills. About 30 counties were entirely 
covered by special school taxing districts. As the usual 
county-wide tax levy was 11.5 mills, which would be in- 
creased in practically all special school districts to 14.5, it 
will be seen that in the greater part of the state, educa- 
tion was absorbing almost half its revenue from ad valo-

The problems of property taxation in 1931 are much 
the same as today. Issues such as fair appraisals, equal 
application of the appraisal process and restrictions on 
the number of mills that could be levied are current to- 
day. Even federal court intervention has failed to quiet

in Educational Finance, 1932. In its review 
of Alabama's financial situation, the Brookings Report of 
1932 defined the shortcomings of Alabama's system for 
the support of public schools: 


A-218 


The reliance that is placed in Alabama on ad 
valorem taxation for the financing of the public 
schools places a disproportionate share of the 
burden of educational support on the owners of 
property. The entire system, including the 
method by which the taxes are levied, has other 
conspicuous shortcomings. 

Embodied in the Constitution, it lacks the 
flexibility necessary for adjustments to condi- 
tions, which change from year to year and vary 
from county to county and from district to dis- 
trict. Since local taxes are subject to popular 
approval, it endows the electorate with a bud- 
getary and appropriation function with respect 
to a single field of administration. Voters can 
hardly be expected to judge intelligently the 
educational needs of an entire county. The mat- 
ter is entirely too complicated, particularly 
where a majority of voters are non-taxpayers 
and for that reason are likely to feel little re- 
sponsibility for the taxes that they vote. But, at 
the best, no intelligent action is possible with 
respect to one field of administration unless the 
needs of all other fields are at the same time 
taken into account. The present system permits, 
in general, no comparison between the needs of 
education and the needs of other functions, 
such as law enforcement, health, welfare, and 
highways. 

Moreover, an indispensable feature of a 
proper appropriating system, is that appropria- 
tions should be subjected to periodical review. 
If the electorate is competent to vote on appro- 
priations, it should do so oftener than once ev- 
ery ten, twenty, or forty years. Yet, no one would 
seriously propose the submission of school tax 
levies annual or biennially to the county or dis- 
trict electorate. 


A-219 


By earmarking taxes for specific purposes, the 
plan now prescribed by the Constitution makes 
necessary an excessive number of special funds, 
complicates and confuses accounting, and ren- 


ders impossible any clear understanding of the 
cost and needs of schools. 


It is fully realized that the present system has 
not been without its advantages in the educa- 
tional development of Alabama. It is believed, 
however, that the time has come for a more 
flexible system in financing the public schools 
and one more in conformity with modem prac- 
tice in budgeting and financial control (Brook- 
ings Institute, pp. 276-277). 


The theory of state-aid funds at this time appears to 
have been based on three precepts: 


1. that the public schools could not or should not be 
maintained entirely by local taxation; 

2. that counties should be encouraged to levy the 
maximum constitutional tax rates for school pur- 
poses; and 

3. that substantial equality of educational opportunity 
should be provided for all the children of the state. 


whether resident in the wealthier or in the poorer 
counties. 


The effects of the Depression of 1929 would destroy 
equalization and force nearly total support for the 
schools back on the local communities. Nothing could 
obscure the obvious fact that the state was in deep finan- 


cial trouble and could not afford to support public edu- 
cation, 


The Budget and Financial Control Act of 1932 


On August 16, 1932, the legislature met in extraordi- 
nary session to deal with the problems. Governor Miller 
proposed a program for meeting the deficit and correct- 


A-220 


ing the underlying causes of the continuing deficits. In 
brief, that program embraced the following: (1) the 
funding of the warrants and accounts payable; (2) the 
levying of an income tax; (3) the reduction of appropria- 
tions based upon consolidation of offices and functions 
and upon salary cuts; and (4) improvements in account- 
ing and budgeting, with considerable stress placed on a 
provision to make no appropriation payable in full unless 
there were sufficient funds to meet it (Alyea, p. 16). The 
Budget and Financial Control Act known popularly as 
the “Fletcher Act,” was passed on September 27, 1932. It 
was an ambitious piece of legislation designed to correct 
administrative and legislative inadequacies of long dura- 
tion. Three areas were addressed: (I partial administra- 
tive reorganization, (2) reform of legislative appropria- 
tion practices, and (3) budgetary reform. (Alyea, p. 16). 

The section of this Act which would become a center 
of controversy an disappointment to public education 
deals with appropriations: 


A-221 


cient to pay all of the appropriations in full; the 
Governor shall restrict allotments to prevent an 
overdraft or deficit in any fiscal year for which 
appropriations are made by prorating without 
discrimination against any department, institu- 
tion, commission or other State agency, the 
available revenues among the various depart- 
ments, institutions, bureaus, boards, commis- — 
sions and other State agencies. In other words, 
the said appropriations shall be payable in such 
proportion as the total sum of all appropria- 
tions bears to the total revenues estimated 
the Governor as available in each of said fiscal 
years. The purpose of this provision is to insure 
that there shall be no overdraft or deficit in the 
several funds of the State at the end of a fiscal 
year, and the Governor is directed and required 
to so administer this Act as to prevent any such 
overdrafts or deficits (Acts of Alabama, 1932, 
No. 37, pp. 44-45). 


Section 19.—APPROPRIATIONS: The appro- 
priations made shall not be available for expen- 
diture until allotted as provided for in Section 
20, except as may be otherwise provided in this 
Act. All appropriations now or hereafter made 
except per-capita appropriations now in force 
or hereafter made to eleemosynary and correc- 
tional institutions and the Alabama School for 
Deaf and Blind at,Talladega, Alabama, which 
appropriations shall remain in full force and ef- 
fect and payable and disbursed as now provided 
by law are hereby declared to be maximum, 
conditional and proportionate appropriations, 
the purpose being to make the appropriations 
payable in full in the amounts named only in 
the event that the estimated budget resources 
during each fiscal year of the quadrennium for 
which such appropriations are made, are suffi- 


Thus, Section 19 provided that appropriations, with a 
few stated exceptions, are not available for expenditure 
until allotted. It became the function of the governor to 
prevent any threatened overdraft. Appropriations were to 
be apportioned so that the money actually appropriated 
to any state department is in the same proportion to the 
total appropriation as the budgeted amount is to the 
Governor’s estimate of total available revenue. 


During the early years this proved to be the 
most single important provision in the Fletcher 
Act; in fact, in the minds of many Alabamians, it 
is the budget act. . . . This formula at first glance 
appears to be a perfectly clear one, requiring 
little or no interpretation. However, such did 
not prove to be the case. The relatively exten- 
sive litigation and the subsequent effects of judi- 
cial interpretations of this provision proved to 


A-222 


be exceedingly important in the years immedi- 
ately following (Alyea, p. 19). 


Proration, 1932. The legislature had not fully understood 
the complexities of putting the state on a 100 percent 
pay-as-you-go plan requiring the scaling down of all ex- 
penditures to fit expected revenues. The comptroller 
made his first budget estimates on November 26, 1932, 
with the following adjustments necessary to the General 
Fund:

On the basis of these figures and the understanding of 
the Budget and Control Act in Section 19, all agencies 
funded through the General Fund would have their ap- 
propriations reduced by 14.26 percent. The ASETF 
would be reduced 16.57 percent. However, since the 
ASETF relied on a transfer from the General Fund, its 
transfer of ‘ $1.5 million would be reduced to $1,286,100, 
increasing its deficit to $1,038,517.08 or 20.86 percent. 
Therefore, in the initial stage, state education dollars 
were prorated twice (Comptroller's Report, November 26, 
1932). 


Attorney General Opinions on Proration, 1932. Unfortu- 
nately, this dilemma was only the beginning. On Decem- 


A-223 


ber 1, 1932, the comptroller wrote the attorney general 
for clarification on the following questions of proration: 


(1) Isa statutory salary subject to proration? 

(2) Are appropriations to departments whose 
only appropriations are for statutory sala- 
ries subject to proration? 

(3) Where a lump sum appropriation to a de- 
partment includes in part statutory salaries, 
is this lump sum appropriation subject to 
proration in whole or part? 

(4) Is the Governor's contingent fund subject 
to proration? 

(5) Is the Governor's interest contingent fund 
subject to proration? 

(6) Is the Governor's emergency fund subject 
to proration? 

(7) Does the provision of the Fletcher Act ex- 
cepting per capita appropriations to elee- 
mosynary and correctional institutions in- 
clude the Convict Department? The State 
Training School for Girls? 


With the single exception of question No. 3, the an- 
swer from the attorney general was no (Attorney General's 
Report, October 1, 1932 - September 30, 1934, pp. 101- 
103). 

The result of this ruling was that there were now two 
classes of appropriations—those which were fixed and 
not subject to proration, and those which were subject to 
proration. As a result, the $1.5 million due to the ASETF 
from the General Fund became $862,950, a reduction of 
42.67 percent which, in turn, reduced the total ASETF to 
29.35 percent of its appropriation. 

As the comptroller continued to make further esti- 
mates of revenue, it became clear that the revenue pre- 
dictions of November 26, 1932, could not be reached. A 
further opinion was asked of the attorney general by the 
comptroller “to determine those functions which must 


A-224 


be maintained.” The attorney general replied on May 1, 
1933, by constructing a classification of essential and 
nonessential services of government, even though the 
Fletcher Act made no such reference: 


“... essential functions of government are the 
protection of life, liberty, and property; the or- 
derly settlement of disputes between the sub- 
jects by Courts properly constituted, the sup- 
pression of domestic disorders and the repel- 
ling of foreign invasions; and also the punish- 
ment of crime and the punishment for the vio- 
lation of statutes regulating human conduct (At 


torney General Reports, 1932-34, pp. 220-223).

ernment spending for 1932-33. It is interesting that one 
area, the judicial branch, actually received over 100 per- 
cent of its appropriations, while the Public School Fund 
and the ASETF received only 9.25 percent of their appro- 
priations.

Source: Alabama's Balanang Budget, by Paul Alyea, 1942, p.36. 


Significant outcomes of the first year of operation un- 
der the Budget and Financial Control Act included a 
balanced budget, the establishment of Statutory salaries 
not being subject to proration, and the distinction be- 
tween essential and nonessential functions of state gov- 
ernment. The disaster for education, after suffering 
through inadequate funding already due to the Depres- 
sion of 1929, was that it was deemed a nonessential func- 
tion of state government. Perhaps the greater tragedy of 
this was that the legislature abdicated through the Bud- 
get and Financial Control Act the proper responsibility 
to determine for itself the essential and nonessential 
functions of state government. Instead, such a major re- 
sponsibility fell to a single executive officer, the attorney 
general (Alyea, pp. 37-40). However, the decision of the 
state attorney general would not go unchallenged. 


A-226 


State Supreme Court Decision on Proration: Abramson v. 
Hard, 1932. The policy for making deficiency allotments 
under Section 19 of the Budget and Financial Control 
Act as construed by the attorney general and executed by 
the comptroller was challenged in a taxpayer's bill by W. 
A. Abramson and Elizabeth Baldwin Hill against J. H. 
Hard, Jr., comptroller, and S.H. Blan, state treasurer. 
Abramson and Hill sought to enjoin the state comptrol- 
ler from drawing, and the treasurer from paying, war- 
rants on the balance of general funds until warrants were 
drawn for certain educational appropriations in the 
amount of their pro rata share of revenues available. 
Judge Walter B. Jones of Montgomery County Circuit 
Court, denied this injunction. When appeal was filed 
with the Alabama Supreme Court, the justices all de- 
clared that none of them was competent to sit on the 
case. This disqualification was because proration made 
according to the statutory formula of the Fletcher Act, 
strictly constructed, would necessarily reduce the salary 
of every official of the state, including Supreme Court 
justices. The governor then appointed a special court 
consisting of five members of the bar to hear the case 
(Abramson v. Hard, 229 Ala. 3, 155, So. 590). 

This special Supreme Court also denied the injunc- 


tion: 


To fixed salaries and to other fixed expenses, 
such as the per them allowed to officers for trav- 
eling expenses, both of which are appropria- 
tions to the officers to whom they are due 
rather than to any department, institution, bu- 
reau, board, commission, or other state agency, 
the proration provisions of this act have no ap- 
plication... 

There are, however, some of the necessary ex- 
penses of government which in their nature 
cannot be definitely determined with exactness 


A-227 


in advance, as to which the Legislature has evi- 
denced its intent in our opinion, that they shall 
be paid in full, and to that end has set apart out 
of the general funds an amount which is to be 
used insofar as it is needed to pay these ex- 
peases in full. Appropriations for the payment 
of such governmental expenses do not differ es- 
sentially from fixed salaries or other fixed ex- 
penses. They are to be paid in full, so far as 
needed for payment of the specific governmen- 
tal expenses for which they have been appropri- 
ated. Such expenses as the expense for fuel, 
light, and water, for postage, post office box 
rent, for repairing and insuring state property, 
for public printing, for telephone and telegraph 
service, for publishing the Governor’s proclama- 
tion, for premiums on officials bonds, for dis- 
tributing public documents, for feeding prison- 
ers, for arrest of absconding felons, for removal 
of prisoners, and others that might be men- 
tioned are of this character, and it seems clear 
that the appropriations made for such specific 
purposes were intended by the Legislature to be 
paid in full and the appropriations to their full 
extent here set apart and were intended to be 
used so far as needed to pay them in full. On 
similar principles those appropriations setting 
apart specific amounts to take care of interest 
on the public debt and to provide for contin- 
gencies and emergencies were intended to set 
apart the specific amount fixed therein to be 
used for the purposes specified insofar as they 
are needed. 

No appropriation of this character is, in our 
Opinion an appropriation to any department, 
insutution, bureau, board, commission, or state 
agency “for the administration, operation, or 
maintenance” thereof within the meaning of 


A-228 


the Budget Act so as to be subject to allotment 
and proration. (Abramson v. Hard, 229, Ala.,11.) 


All of the special justices concurred in upholding the 
lower court's decision and, in effect, affirmed the distinc- 
tion previously made between essential and nonessential 
functions of state government. In 1935, the legislature 
would attempt to make education an essential function 
through the Tucker Act, a discussion of which will follow. 


Refinancing State Government, 1932 


Governor Miller, having received the Brookings Re- 
port on the condition of state government on June 11, 
1932, accepted the desperate financial plight of the state. 
The time for action had come. In the special session of 
August 16, 1932, he presented the legislature a plan for 
refunding the state debt: 


We must face facts and not fancy screens. The 
current debts of Alabama have reached and 
d the Twenty Million Dollar Mark. The 
credit of the State must not be lost. The debts 
must be paid or arrangements made to pay 
them. They have been accumulating yearly for 
nearly for over a quarter of a century—since 
about the year 1907. There has been in recent 
years no budget balancing. The appropriations 
grew larger year by year and the income failed 
to keep step; hence the gradual growth of the 
deficit. Each Legislature appropriated more 
than the revenue produced thereby increasing 
appropriations and decreasing revenue annu- 
ally and causing a growing deficit. Banks call it 
overdrafts. 

This must end. The debts must be paid. The 
budget must be balanced. For the debts you and 
the Executive are responsible only in a small 
degree; but we are not met face to face with the 
responsibility to pay or arrange to pay the debts, 


A-229 


and to balance the budget, so we can live within 
our income (Miller, August 16, 1932, p. III). 


Noting that ad valorem taxation had been the primary 
source of government revenues in the post, Governor 
Miller stipulated that no such additional levies should be 
added; instead, he proposed that such levies be lowered 
in the future. Searching for other forms of taxation, he 
discussed a sales tax on all retail sales. He declared this 
form of taxation to be a consumer's tax on those now 
“battling for bread, hunting for food, seeking clothes.” 

Rejecting the sales tax, he proposed that to raise taxes, 
the legislature should go to those who have made money, 
who had money, and who had the ability to pay. Profits 
and incomes had never been taxed in Alabama. Gover- 
_ Miller noted that although there had been no fed- 
eral income tax until 1916, since its inception a total of 
$133,067,985 had been paid by Siitenth tnneiatiens 
and individuals to the federal government. He proposed 
an income tax in Alabama modeled after the federal in- 
come tax which could, in a few years, pay off the current 
debts, assist in running the schools, and gradually reduce 
the ad valorem tax on real and personal property (Miller 
August 16, 1932, pp. V-VI). 

To accomplish this, Governor Miller proposed a consti- 
tutional amendment to authorize a 20 million dollar 
bond issue to pay off the current debts of Alabama as of 
October 1, 1932, and an income tax amendment to pay 
the annual interest on this debt and the balance to 
supplement the educational trust funds for school pur- 


poses. He stressed the need for the income tax for educa- 
tion: 


Alabama's greatest asset is her children. Fif- 
teen Million Dollars practically of this Twenty 
rape ager debt is due to education. Nearly 

ve million of this debt is due th 
en hae a e the teachers for 


A-230 


These debts must be paid. My plan for paying 
and arranging to pay them has been fully stated 


to you. 


A-231 


thereafter, and to designate and to define the 
incomes to be taxed and to fix the rates of taxes 
(Acts of Alabama, Extra Session 1932, No. 8, p. 


8). 


The act was approved on August 30, 1932, and submit- 
ted to the voters for referendum on November 8, 1932, 
just four days after the legislature adjourned. The mea- 
sure was defeated by the people. On January 31, 1933, 
Governor Miller again called the legislature into Special 
Session to consider a new amendment: 


The Educational Trust Fund taxes now aggre- 
gate an annual income for school purposes of 
practically Six Million Dollars. If this Six Million 
Dollar annual school trust fund is used to pay 
the Fifteen Million Dollar school debts, then it 
will take two and one-half years to pay it and 
during that time the schools would receive no 
state aid and would close or shorten their ses- 


sions. If the income tax and bond amendments 
are submitted and approved this will arrange to 
pay the entire school debts, add considerable 
sum to the school fund, and leave the Six Mil- 
lion Dollar Educational Trust Fund free and 
clear from the school. Reduction in salaries, ap- 
propriations and abolishing useless officers, em- 
ployees and departments will lift some of the 
burden off the General Fund and permit the 
schools to participate therein (Miller, August 
16, 1932, p. VII). 


In addition, the governor proposed budgetary control 
measures that were embodied in the Budget and Finan- 
cial Control Act, discussed earlier. 


Income Tax Amendment, 1932. One of the most signifi- 
cant efforts to finance the state came in 1932, when the 
legislature approved an act proposing amendment to the 
Constitution to levy an income tax: 


The Legislature shall have the power to levy 
and collect taxes for State purposes on net in- 
come from whatever source derived within this 
State including the incomes derived from sala- 
ries, fees, and compensation paid by the State, 
County, Municipality, and any agency or crea- 
ture thereof for the calendar year 1932, and 


. . . -[he plans proposed and provided by 
you—an income tax and bond issue amend- 
ments to the Constitution—to pay these debts 
and to keep schools from closing, were defeated 
by the voters at the polls on the 8th day of No- 
vember, 1932. 

This in effect was a repudiation of the debts 
and the plan of payment; but it was not, in my 
opinion, so intended by the electorate. The real 
issue was not made clear and plain to them. 
Much false propaganda mislead them. They had 
no ume to consider it. You adjourned at mid- 
night on the 4th of November and the election 
was on the 8th of November. You could not in 
that time—3 days—present the facts to the vot- 
ers. 

The voters had only one chance at the in- 
come tax amendment and this was four days 
after you adjourned. They should have another 
right and opportunity to pass on it, more than 
ninety days after you adjourn, as the Constitu- 
tion directs. Give the voters an opportunity to 
see the real issue, to realize the struggle “the 
able to pay taxes” are making to avoid paying 
their reasonable share of the taxes and to keep 
the burden of taxes on those “unable to pay it 
(Miller, January 31, 1933, pp. V-V1).” 


A-232 


On April 7, 1933, the legislature approved an income 
tax amendment which was significantly different from 
the one previously submitted: (1) whereas the former did 
not fix the rate of taxation, the latter fixed the rate for 
individuals at five percent and for corporations at three 
percent; (2) whereas the former did not provide for ex- 
emptions, the latter provided exemptions in the amount 
of $1,500 for an unmarried person, $3,000 for a married 
couple, and $300 for each child under 18 years of age 
(Acts of 1933, Extra Session, No. 125, p. 116). These 
conditions made the income tax more acceptable to the 
voters, and with the deteriorating financial condition of 
education in the state, Amendment 25 was passed and 
proclaimed ratified July 18, 1933. The amendment fol- 
lows: 

Article XXII. The Legislature shall have the

A-235 


Warrant Amendment, 1932. The failure of past legisla- 
tures to properly budget expenditures and raise suffi- 
cient revenues to pay appropriations resulted in a cumu- 
lative excess debt over assets of $13,096,633.27 by Sep- 
tember 30, 1931. The borrowings of the teachers’ col- 
leges, the A & M Institute, and the Alabama School of 
Trades added an additional $906,733.22 to this floating 
debt. Added to this sum, the anticipated General Fund 
deficit of $6,419,340 for the 1931-32 fiscal year resulted 
in a total floating debt of $20,422,706.49, which was re- 
quired by law to be liquidated by September 30, 1932 
(Brookings Institute, Vol. 3, p. 20). 

The incurrence and administration of debt by the state 
had been addressed in Article XI, Section 213, of the 
Constitution of 1901: 


After the ratification of this Constitution no 
new debt shall be created against or incurred by 
this State, or its authority, except to repel inva- 
sion or suppress insurrection, and then only by 
a concurrence of two-thirds of the members of 
each House of the Legislature, and the vote 
shall be taken by yeas and nays, and entered on 
the Journals; and any act creating or incurring 
any new debt against this State, except as herein 
provided for, shall be absolutely void; provided 
the governor may be authorized to negotiate 
temporary loans never to exceed three hundred 
thousand dollars, to meet the deficiencies in the 
treasury, and until the same is paid no new loan 
shall be negotiated; provided further that this 
section shall not be so construed as to prevent 
the issuance of bonds for the purpose of re- 
funding the existing bonded indebtedness of 
the State (Constitution of 190 1, Article XI, Sec- 
tion 213). 


A-236 


Under these provisions, it appears certain that the float- 
ing debt was contrary to the intent of the constitution. 
However, by using the $300,000 authorized to the 
governor's office, the governor had been able to pay the 
interest and renew the debt yearly. 

As previously stated, a direct cause of the floating debt 
had been the creation of the ASETF in 1927. The 1927 
legislature had doubts as to whether the taxes levied in 
the creation of the ASETF would be adequate to fully 
fund the continuing provisions of the Unified Education 
Budget of 1927 (Act 1927, Regular Session, No. 382, p. 
445). In anticipation of this shortfall, the legislature had 
provided for relief from the General Fund: 


Section 31. The several appropriations herein 
provided for shall be payable out of the special 
trust fund created and set apart for educational 
purposes by Section 2 of the General Revenue 
Bill of 1927; and in the event such trust fund is 
insufficient to cover said appropriations, then 
the balance of the same shall be payable out of 
any other funds in the treasury not otherwise 
appropriated (Acts 1927, Regular Session, No. 
382, p. 442). 


It is clear that the educational aims of the 1927 legisla- 
ture were not adequately funded through new revenues 
and thus became the major component of the floating 


debt. 
To pay off the floating debt of the state and reestablish 


financial integrity, in 1933 the legislature passed a consti- 
tutional amendment to accompany the income tax 
amendment, which would provide state government with 
the proceeds of the income tax and legally repay all 
loans. The so-called “Warrant Amendment” was pre- 
sented to the people on July 18, 1933, and proclaimed 
ratified as Amendment 26 on August 2, 1933: 


A-237 


After the ratification of this constitution, no 
new debt shall be created against, or incurred 
by the state, or its authority except to repel inva- 
sion or suppress insurrection, and then only by 
a concurrence of two-thirds of the members of 
each house of the legislature, and the vote shall 
be taken by yeas and nays and entered on the 
journals; provided, the governor may be autho- 
rized to negotiate temporary loans, never to ex- 
ceed three hundred thousand dollars to meet 
the deficiencies in the treasury, and until the 
same is paid no new loan shall be negotiated; 
(provided, further, that this section shall not be 
so construed as to prevent the issuance of bonds 
for the purpose of refunding the existing 
bonded indebtedness of the state. Provided, fur- 
ther, that this section shall not be so construed 
as to prevent the governor from paying interest 
at the rate of not exceeding 5% per annum pay- 
able semiannually from July 1, 1933, on the 
floating indebtedness of the state at the close of 
business on September 30, 1932, as shown by 
outstanding and unpaid warrants drawn on the 
treasury, as provided by law, amounting in the 
aggregate to $16,943,357.12 and items enumer- 
ated in an act of the legislature number 294, 
being senate bill 272, approved November 9, 
1932 (Acts 1932, Ex. Session., p. 298), all of 
which are hereby ratified and confirmed.) All 
warrants and/or instruments issued or to be is- 
sued representing such indebtedness shall be a 
direct obligation of the state, and for the 
prompt and faithful payment of the principal 
and interest thereon, the full faith and credit of 
the state is hereby irrevocably pledged, and 
such warrants and/or instruments shall be ex- 
empt forever from ali taxes of every kind. Any 
act creating or incurring any new debt against 
the state, except as herein provided for, shall be 


A-238 


absolutely void. To create a sinking fund for the 
prompt and faithful payment of the floating in- 
debtedness of the state, and interest thereon, 
the net proceeds of any income tax which may 
be levied by the legislature pursuant to law is 
hereby pledged. To prevent further deficits in 
the state treasury, it shall be unlawful from and 
after the adoption of this amendment for the 
state comptroller of the state of Alabama to 
draw any warrant or other order for the pay- 
ment of money belonging to, or administered 
by, the state of Alabama upon the state trea- 
surer, unless there is in the hand of such trea- 
surer money appropriated and available for the 
fan payment of the same. In case there is, at the 
end of any fiscal year, insufficient money in the 
State treasury for the payment of all proper 
claims presented to the state comptroller for 
the issuance of warrants, the comptroller shall 
issue warrants for that proportion of each such 
claim which the money available for the pay- 
ment of all said claims bears to the whole, and 
such warrants for such prorated sums shall 
thereupon be paid by the state treasurer. At the 
end of each fiscal year all unpaid appropriations 
which exceed the amount of money in the state 
treasury subject to the payment of the same af- 
ter the proration above provided for, shall 
thereupon become null and void to the extent 
of such excess. Any person violating any of the 
provisions of this amendment shall, on convic- 
tion, be punished by a fine of not exceeding five 
thousand dollars, or by imprisonment in the 
penitentiary for not more thai two years, one or 
both, at the discretion of the jury trying the 
same, and the violation of any provisions of this 
amendment shall also be ground for impeach- 
ment (Amendment 26, Constitution of 1901). 


A-239 


Depression Disbursements. After a century of statehood, 
a financial house of cards had collapsed: 


It was not until the great depression of the 
early nineteen-thirties that the State was forced 
into a thorough-going financial readjustment 


(Ray, p. 84). 


By 1930 predepression disbursements had peaked. As 
shown in Table 4-4, if the total disbursements for 1930 
are taken as a base at 100 percent then disbursements 
stood at 69.4 percent in 1932 and at 66.0 percent in 
1933. By 1935, they had recovered to 83.1 percent. The 
Budget and Financial Control Act had begun operation 
in 1933 and was the last nail in the coffin of the floating 
debt. While many General Fund and ASETF disburse- 
ments were reduced drastically in 1933, legislative and 
judicial disbursements were not affected by the depres- 
sion. Health disbursements, as well as educational ones, 
were declared “nonessential” and bore the full brunt of 
proration. But even before the depression, the growth of 
schools in Alabama had not been rapid. Both govern- 
ment leaders and the people regarded education as a 
burden, and only meager support for it was gained after 
World War I. As a result of this attitude, by 1930 every 
aspect of education in Alabama had come to depend 
heavily on state funding, and this dependence aggravated 
the financial blow of having over one-half of these funds 
withdrawn in the course of three years (Ray, p. 85-89). 

It i8\fair to say that schools in Alabama during the 
1930s suffered not so much from discriminatory prora- 
tion, as from the fact that so much state funding came 
from the ASETF. The reduction in disbursements for 
school purposes from 1932 to 1933 exceeded $5.5 mil- 
lion or nearly $500,000 more than the total 1930 dis- 
bursements to the other five areas of state government as 
shown in Table 4-4. 


A-240 
TABLE 4-4

While it is correct that schools suffered the greatest 
dollar loss during the depression, Table 4-4 also illus- 
trates that other areas of state government were penal- 
ized to a greater degree in terms of percentage. The 
stabilizing influence of the Public School Fund assisted 
schools greatly during the depression and prevented the 
total disbursement from falling lower than it did. Table 
4-5 shows the rapid recovery of the Public School Fund 
by 1935, while other categories such as higher education, 
the Equalization Fund, and the Attendance Fund contin- 
ued to face financial ruin. By 1935, some items in Table 
4-5 received no funding, but were absorbed into other 
amounts. 

From the depression disbursement experience, it can 
be concluded that many of the reductions occurred 
through the contraction of revenues earmarked for par- 
ticular functions and services declared to be nonessen- 
tial. The result of the experience was renewed impetus 
for earmarking of revenues, with agencies and services 
preferring since the depression to avoid dependence on 
the General Fund. Of the tax changes made from 1930 to 


A-241 


the levy of the sales tax in 1936-37, only the proceeds of 
the inheritance tax were earmarked for the General 
Fund. Other revenues were reserved for other uses: the 
gasoline tax for highways, tobacco and sales taxes for 
education, mileage taxes for the motor carrier fund, and 
the income tax for service of warrant refunding bonds. 
Thus, the advantages of new taxes precipitated by the 
depression accrued to highways, education, and debt ser- 
vice. Distrust of the administration may be less of an un- 
derlying cause of excessive earmarking than the desire of 
agency administrators to achieve independence from the 
legislature (Ray, pp. 91-99).

Effects of Refinancing Measures, 1932-34. The Warrant 
and Income Tax Amendments were evidence that the 
people of Alabama were willing to see that the state met 
its just obligations. While most of the $20 million bond 
issue thus authorized was to pay state debts for educa- 
tion, the bond issue did nothing for the 1934 status of 
educational financing. Paying off old debts did not allevi- 
ate the lack of funding under the Budget and Financial 
Control Act. State Superintendent A.F. Harmon charac- 
terized the situation in 1932-33: “So far as education is 
concerned, financial chaos more aptly describes our con- 
dition. The financing of education in Alabama is far 
from a settled question. More is the wonder, and great 
should be the praise for all those who had their share in 
it, that our public school system has not completely bro- 
ken down. Their endurance passeth understanding (An- 
nual Report, 1933, Part I p. 18).” 

For the 1932-33 school year, only 46 percent of the 
ASETF appropriations and 71 percent of the anticipated 
revenues from the Public School Fund were paid by the 
state. As a result of the Budget and Financial Control Act, 
these unpaid appropriations ceased to be an obligation 
of the state. The efforts to create a minimum school term 
of seven months were wasted as Alabama looked forward 
to an average elementary school term of four months 
and an average high school term of four and one-half 
months for 1933-34. Only with financial aid from the 
Federal Emergency Relief Administration to supplement 
local, county, and state funds for rural elementary 
schools was there a school term at all (Annual Report, 
1933, Part I, pp. 18-21). By the 1933-34 school year, con- 
ditions had not improved. It appeared that only 58 per- 
cent of the ASETF appropriations would be available to 
the schools. Concern continued to grow over the effects 
of the Budget and Financial Control Act: 


A-243 


The Budget and Financial Control Act is 
sound in principle. It does not solve the prob- 
lem of financing education in Alabama. To 
solve the problem of financing education, rev- 
enues must be provided to pay appropriations 
in full, laws must be enacted to place education 
among the essential functions of government 
and the budget act must be revised to make 
possible sound budgetary procedure with re- 
neha education (Annual Report, 1934, Part l, 
p. 41). 


As is seen in Table 4-6 below, conditions in education, 
which had improved from the Unified Educational Pro-

rated. The term length had decreased to 128 days, well 
below the 140-day minimum term expected. The expen- 
diture per pupil and teachers’ salaries declined drasti- 


cally.

Temporary Federal Relief. It was abundantly clear that 
the three major legislative efforts of the 193] quad- 
rennium, the Budget and Financial Control Act, the War- 


A-244 


rant Amendment, and the Income Tax Amendment had 
not solved the financial plight of education. Only one 
source of financial relief could be found, the Federal 
Emergency Relief Administration. As the 1934 Annual Re 
port shows, relief was badly needed: 


During 1932-33 many Alabama schools were 
forced to close abruptly in the midst of the term 
because of lack of revenues. The school term 
for the state for that year was only 128 days. 


Many teachers went unpaid during a part of 
that time. Again in 1933-34 schools faced the 


year with such limited finances that there was 
little prospect for a normal term in many coun- 
ties and cities, no matter how drastically ex- 
penses were curtailed (Annual Report, 1934, Part 
I, pp. 51-52). 


By January of 1934, it was clear that all schools would 
be closed in 24 counties and in 12 cities. Under the pro- 
visions of the Federal Emergency Relief Administration, 
school terms could be extended with federal funds in 
counties and cities under 5,000 population if all state and 
local funds were exhausted. Only teachers whose salaries 
comprised their sole source of income were eligible for 
employment. Federal relief funds in the amount of 
$1,971,982.21 were actually used in extending school 
terms. In all, some 39 of the 67 counties, 22 of the 24 
cities under 5,000 population, and the 11 state secondary 
agricultural schools used federal relief funds for payment 
of salaries for teachers for periods extending from six 
days to 4.9 months (Annual Report, 1934, Part 1, pp. 5 
153). But this relief was only a temporary stopgap mea- 
sure and would not be available in the next academic 
year. The worst conditions ever to befall education in 
Alabama were just around the corner.

Bibb Graves Re-elected, 1935 


Bibb Graves was inaugurated as governor for the sec- 
ond time on January 14, 1935. However, it was his prede- 
cessor, Governor Miller who addressed the legislature on 
January 8, 1935, for the opening session of the 1935 
quadrennium. Miller recounted the Budget and Finan- 
cial Control Act, the Income Tax Amendment, and the 
Warrant Amendment as great achievements of his admin- 
istration and the people of Alabama. He noted that the 
state budget was balanced, that there was a cash balance 
in the treasury, and enough anticipated revenues “if eco- 


nomically administered, to defray the expenses of the 
government.” 


_ The educational financial trouble in Alabama 
is not due to the State but grows out of the fact 
that some of the County, district and City 
Boards of Education have anticipated and spent 
the County, District and City taxes for years be- 
fore they were received. Beautiful buildings 
were erected on credit and the future taxes 
were pledged to meet annually the interest and 
principal on the building debts. When the 
County, District and City taxes were collected, 
in many instances they are applied to the pay- 


A-246 


ment of building and school bus debts and the 
teachers go unpaid on account thereof. 

An annual budget balancing system with 
teeth in it and penalties attached should be 
made applicable to these county and city boards 
of education, just like it applies to the State of 
Alabama. When that is done you can expect 
business results from our Educational system 
and teachers will be paid their salaries (Miller, 
January 8, 1935, pp. XVI-XVIII). 


A different tone, however, was set in the inauguration 
speech of Governor Bibb Graves. He promised a progres- 
sive administration that would stress human welfare as 
the primary objective of his administration: 


Human welfare is the fixed star that sets the 
course of America’s ship of state. That ship s 
greatest captain, Franklin D. Roosevelt, with 
clear eye and firm hand, is keeping us stead- 
fastly on the course and that course is the only 
sure way to peace and safety, to prosperity and 
happiness, at and abroad (Alabama Journal, 
Montgomery, Alabama, January 14, 1935, p. 
38). 


In his address to the legislature on May 7, 1935, Gover-

We advocate the continuous responsibility of 
government for human welfare and especially 
the protection of children (Graves, May 7, 1935, 
P. xiii). 

He continued that “We must provide for Alabama a 
functioning government with stress on schools and 
health.” In order to provide adequate financing for state 
government and education Graves asked the legislature 
to pass a revenue bill during the session beginning May 7 
and to postpone appropriations until after a recess. It was 


A-247 


his feeling that a more intelligent decision on financial

The Financial Conditions of Education, 1935-36 


With the failure of the Tucker Act, an attempt to end 
the financial discrimination against education, in 1935, 
the excessive proration under the Budget and Financial 
Control Act continued as it had since 1932. Although 
three major acts would be passed during the August 16, 
1935, session creating a minimum foundation program 
and improved local budgeting, none of these measures 
would increase funding. And even with increased fund- 
ing, without earmarking, such funds would be subject to 
the proration because education had been ruled a nones- 
sential function of state government. 

By February and March of 1936, schools in 51 counties 
had closed early with less than a seven-month school 
term. As late as February, 1936, teachers in many coun- 
ties had received only one month's salary (Cain, p. 237). 
The American Legion vigorously opposed the manner in 
which state funds were being prorated, calling it a great 
injustice to pay some state employees one hundred per- 
cent while leaving schools and teachers to beg: 


Education is essential to democratic govern- 
ment. Ignorance is the most potent ally of au- 
tocracy, communism and other “isms’ which are 
ever on the alert to tear down the principles for 
which we fought in '17 and '18 and for which 
our greatest organization stands. .. . The public 
schools are the chief bulwark to preserve the 
principles of Americanism. We cannot and we 
must not let our schools close (Alabama School 
Journal, Vol. LI, Feb. 1936, p. 7). 


The report to the governor from the comptroller on Feb- 
ruary 10, 1936, revealed the desperate financial condi- 


A-248 


tion of the schools, listing the following appropriations 
due from the General Fund for education:

In addition, $541,650 had been appropriated for mis- 
cellaneous nonessentials such as health for a grand total 
of $6,433,357.61 for nonessential functions. Revenue was 
anticipated to be $7,735,816.77 with essential functions 
claiming $6,035,384.72 and leaving a balance for nones- 
sential functions as defined by Abramson v. Hard in the 
amount of $1,700,432.05. This meant that education 
would receive only 26.43 percent of the amount the legis- 
lature had appropriated and deemed necessary to oper- 
ate the schools. The education appropriation was thus 
reduced by $4,334,463.65 (Graves, February 11, 1936, pp.

the legislature on February 11, 1936: 


I believe that we face an early collapse of our 
school and health programs unless effective fi- 
nancial relief is promptly provided. To provide 
this relief is the great purpose of this session 
(Graves, February 11, 1936, p. III). 


As the 1935-36 financial year closed, the public schools 
were limited to a state minimum program term of only 
five and three-quarters months, probably the shortest in 
the nation. Ninety-two percent of the state’s deficit was 
again assessed against public education. Thousands of 
school children failed to be promoted during this year 
because of the short term and were forced to repeat the 
year (Annual Report, 1935-37, Part 1, p. 23). 


A-249 
Parity for Education, 1935-36 


During this period these were two attempts to make 
education a legitimately equal function of government. 
Both attempts ended in failure. 


The Tucker Act, 1935. One such effort was made in the 
legislative session beginning in May 1935. The Tucker 
Act, approved on June 27, 1935, sought to end the dis- 
crimination against educational appropriations as de- 
cided in Abramson v. Hard by declaring education to be 
an essential function of state government. An essential 
function of state government would be paid first from 
available revenues. However, appropriations not paid to 
the schools at the end of the fiscal year would cease to be 
a debt of the state to the schools. The Tucker Act con- 
tained the following provisions: 


Section 1. The maintenance and operation of 
the public schools, secondary schools, normal 
schools, and institutions of higher learning in 
the State of Alabama, is hereby declared to be 
an essential function of State Government of 
equal importance with the other essential func- 
tions of State Government such as the legisla- 
tive, executive and judicial departments; and 
the payment of salaries of the teachers and ad- 
ministrative officers of said schools and institu- 
tions is as essential as the payment of the salary 
of any officer of the State. 


Section 2. In the administration of that cer- 
tain Act known as the Budget and Financial 
Control Act, approved September 27, 1932, and 
of any other law of the State of Alabama, no 
discrimination against in prorating, allotment, 
or payment of appropriations shall be made 
against any of said schools or institutions in fa- 
vor of any other department, board, institution, 


A-250 


commission, or other State agency, or in favor 
of any officer or agent of the State of Alabama, 


whether on a salary fixed by law or not, it being 
the purpose of this act to establish, in the allot- 
ment, proration, and/ or payment of appropria- 
tions for the public schools and educational in- 
stitutions of the State, identity and equality of 
treatment, and equal priority, with the other es- 
sential departments and officers of the State 
Government (Acts of Alabama, 1935, No. 15 1, 


p. 193). 


However, despite the good intentions of this act, it was 
declared unconstitutional. In an opinion of the attorney 
general on December 3, 1935, the inability of the legisla- 
ture to set aside the construction of the law already ap- 
plied by the courts to actual cases (Abramson v. Hard) was 


affirmed: 


A reading of this act leads to the inescapable 
conclusion that it was designed to overrule to 
the extent there indicated, the decision of the 
Court in Abramson v. Hard, supra. This is not 
within the power enjoyed by the legislature. . . . 
The effect of constitutional provisions is not 
thus dependent upon the will of the legislature. 

Act No. 151, approved June 27, 1935, is un- 
constitutional as violative of Sections 42 and 43 
of the Constitution (Attorney General Reports, Oc- 
tober 1, 1935-December 31, 1935, pp. 281-285). 


An attempt to end this discrimination by constitutional 
amendment followed in 1936 in the Harrison Bill. 


The Harrison Bill, 1936. In the wake of the failure of the 
Tucker Act, the legislature sought to pass a parity amend- 
ment to the constitution which would provide funds for 
education on an equal basis with other state functions. 
Strong sentiment existed among the citizens for such an 


A-251 


amendment, and several bills proposing it were intro- 
duced. The Harrison Bill and the Clay Bill were the two 
received most favorably in the legislature. 

The bill which would come to be known as the 
Harrison Bill originated in the House of Representatives 
as House Bill 11 by Kelly. However, Harrison succeeded 
in substituting his bill for the one by Kelly on the floor of 
the House. This bill won House approval and was trans- 
mitted to the Senate. The following excerpt from House 
Bill 11 summarizes the key provisions of the bill: 


Section 1. That an amendment to the Consti- 
tution of the State of Alabama to be known and 
designated as Article XXIV is hereby proposed, 
the proposed amendment being as follows. 

Article XXIV. Public education is hereby de- 
clared to be an essential and primary function 
of government in this State. It shall be unlawful 
for the Comptroller or like officer of the State 
of Alabama to draw any warrant or other order 
for the payment of money belonging to or ad- 
ministered by the State of Alabama upon the 
State Treasurer unless and until it shall have 
been certified to such comptroller or like of- 
ficer by such Treasurer that there is then in the 
hands of such Treasurer money available and 
appropriated for the full payment of the same. 
All appropriations now or hereafter made shall 
be either (a) Absolute, or (b) Conditional. 

(a) The following shall be absolute appropria- 
tions, shall have preference over condi- 
tional appropriations, and to the limit of 
the money in the hands of the Treasurer 
available therefor, shall be payable in fun 
in the order named: 

1. Appropriations for payment of interest 
or principal or sinking fund on the pub- 
lic debt or on trust funds covered into 
the State Treasury. 


A-252 


2. Per capita appropriations to eleemosy- 
nary and correctional institutions and 
the care and maintenance of convicts. 

3. Appropriations to make effective any 
constitutional provision setting apart any 
certain tax, excise, income or charge for 
a designated use or purpose, not includ- 
ing appropriations to be made from the 
State’s General Fund. 

(b) All other appropriations shall be condi- 
tional appropriations and shall be payable 
in full only in the event that after providing 
for payment of the absolute appropriations 
there remains in the hands of the Trea- 
surer money available for the full payment 
of such conditional appropriations, and in 
the case there is insufficient money avail- 
able for such full payment, them the money 
available to the limit of the money in the 
hands of the Treasurer available thereof, 
shall be prorated without any discrimina- 
tion, and each conditional appropriation 
shall be payable in the proportion which 
the money available bears to the total of all 
such conditional appropriations. At the 
end of each fiscal year all umpaid condi- 
tional appropriations which exceed the 
amount of money in the State Treasury 
subject to the payment of the same, after 
such proration, shall thereupon become 
null and void to the extent of such excess 
(Journal of the Senate, 1936, pp. 722-723).

Minimum Program Enacted, 1935 

Bibb Graves’ promise in his 1926 campaign to provide 
4 minimum seven-month term was partially fulfilled 
through the increased revenues provided by the ASETF 
and the Unified Educational Program. However, the in- 
tervention of the depression prohibited the fulfillment of 
the program. During the 1934 campaign, Graves again 
promised a minimum school term of seven months. re- 
quiring both increased funding and a pattern of equal- 
—, While - legislature was receptive in 1935 to 
establishing such a minimum , the enabli . 
islation which followed was pment oe 
for increasing revenue. As has been described, education 
was already incapable of receiving the appropriations 
promised because of the Budget and Financial Control 
Act of 1932. A minimum educational program could not 
be established in every city and county school system 
without the needed revenues. 


A-254 


The legislature met again on August 13, 1935, after a 
recess. On September 2, 1935, the two companion acts 
which comprised the Minimum Program Acts were 
passed. The first of these acts, No. 326, was an appropria- 
tion bill; the second act, No. 295, established the rules 
and regulations by which the appropriated funds would 
be distributed and apportioned. Studies of the 67 county 
and 45 city school systems had indicated that the state 
would have to provide an equalized Minimum Program 
Fund of at least $6 million in addition to the Public 
School Fund to achieve a minimum school term of seven 
months. Unfortunately, the calculations were based on 
outdated depression-era information. The attendance 
data were for 1933-34, and no provision was made for 
growth in attendance or for adjusting teachers’ salaries in 
proportion to training. So in spite of the good intent of 
the legislature of 1935, there was no assurance that the 
funds appropriated would be sufficient (Annual Report, 
1935-37, Part 1. pp. 20-21). 

The seven-month school term was agreed upon as the 
minimum program school term for several reasons. First, 
it had been the goal in 1927 and not reached by 1935. 
Second, the high schools of Alabama traditionally oper- 
ated for nine months when funds were available. Many 
felt that the elementary schools, which traditionally oper- 
ated when funds were available for seven months, should 
operate for nine months also. Nevertheless, the legisla- 
ture could see no way to support high schools for a nine- 
month term and to lengthen the seven-month elemen- 
tary school term to nine months. Therefore, the compro- 
mise was a minimum term of seven months for both (An- 

nual Report, 1935-37, Part 1, p. 22). 


Act of the Minimum Program, 1935. Act 
Number 326, which provided the financial basis for the 
Minimum Program, had numerous shortcornings: 


A-255 


1. The total Minimum Program Fund appropriation of 


$6,042,516 was inadequate the day j appropri 
t was 
because it was calculated from reeves data. yoga 


2. The additional appropriation of $3,160,516 was a con- 


4. The appropriations previously made in a separate cat- 
ony to equalize educational Opportunity in the Uni- 
Educational Program of 1927 were combined into 

one appropriation in the Minimum Program Fund. 

5. The appropriations to the Minimum Program Fund 
the Revolving Fund, and the State Secondary Agricul 
tural School Fund were to be made from the ASETF 
when available. Of these appropriations, the only 
guaranteed revenues were those from taxes ear- 
marked to the ASETF and not subject to the Budget 

_ and Financial Control Act. . 

6. The Minimum Program Fund is incorrectly called a 
fund since it has no revenue of its own, only those of 
the ASETF, the Public School Fund, and the General 
Fund (when available), and the Public School Fund. 


Due to the financial constraints of in 
priations and limited allotments ater pag rns ane 
Financial Control Act, the Minimum Program had no 
chance of being fully implemented as called for in its 
companion bill, No. 295. As long as the total revenues of 
the state were inadequate to fully fund the essential func- 
tions of State government first, there was no chance that 
the Minimum Program Fund could receive its full appro- 
priation. And with the depression still being felt in Ala- 


A-256 


bama, there was no economic recovery in sight that 
would provide enough revenues through the taxes levied 
by the state government to fully fund appropriations. 
The only alternative open for providing equity to educa- 
tion was through additional earmarking of funds until 
sufficient revenues were generated to equal the total ap- 
propriations made by the legislature. Only the Previously 
earmarked revenues from the Constitution of 1901 and 
the ASETF of 1927 had saved the schools from complete 
financial collapse during the depression. Earmarking be- 
came the only solution because both statutory and consti- 
tutional attempts at parity had failed. 


Apportionment Act of the Minimum Program, 1935. The 
apportionment of the appropriations to the Minimum 
Program Fund was to be done so as to promote equaliza- 
tion of educational opportunity. The promotion of 
equalization was a very difficult matter in Alabama in 
1935 because of the wide variety of local conditions 


which existed: 


1. The assessed valuation of property per child 
enrolled varied from $546 in the poorest 
county to $2,651 in the wealthiest county. 

~ 2. The percentage of Negro children enrolled 
to the total enrollment of negroes and whites 
varied from 0.2 of I per cent in one county to 
87 per cent in another county. 

3. The density of white children per square 
mile varied from 0.8 of a student in the most 
sparsely settled county to 18 in the most 
densely populated county. 

4. Transportation and consolidation were al- 
most completely developed in some counties 
while little had been accomplished in a few 
counties. 


A-257 


5. The percentage of college graduates among 
white teachers varied from 10 per cent in 
one county to 60 per cent in another county. 

6. The existing state appropriations were inad- 
equate and even these were not paid (Annual 
Report, 1935-37, Part I, pp. 18-19). 


It was the purpose of the apportionment act to take 
these factors into consideration and promote equaliza- 
“ion, notwithstanding the reality of item number 6 above 
(nonessentiality and proration). 

In planning the apportionment act, the legislature 
kept the following principles uppermost in mind: 


l. The educational welfare of a child in one sec- 
ion of the State is as important as the educa- 
tional welfare of a child living in any other 
section of the State. 

2. The burden which must be home in support 
of education should be equalized. 

3. Every school unit in the State should be re- 
quired to make a uniform tax effort in order 
to provide the minimum state term. If after 
taxing itself to the uniform level, a school 
unit still lacks sufficient funds to provide the 
minimum term, the remainder of the cost 
should be paid from the State Treasury. 

4. No school system in the State should be tom 
down in order to build up any other system. 
In other words, the State should level up- 
ward, never downward. 

5. Local units should be stimulated to exceed 
the State program whenever possible. 

6. Legitimate educational needs should be sci- 
entifically calculated in order to provide for 
flexibility in educational programs. Further- 


A-258 


7. Provisions should be made for growth in edu- 
cational load. 

8. Sound business procedures should be fol- 
lowed in administration of every school unit 
in the State in order that the children might 
get the maximum benefit per educational 
dollar expended, and 

9. State funds should be apportioned in such a 
manner as to encourage the administrative 
efficiency of local boards of education and to 
discourage inefficiency (2Annual Reports, 
1935-37, p. 20). 


The apportionment act as entitled contained the fol- 
lowing provisions: 

Section I established the Minimum Program Fund to 
include all appropriations made by the legislature to the 
credit of the fund and any other funds set aside for that 
purpose, including the Equalization Fund established in 
1927. 

Section 2 stated two purposes of the act; one, that the 
fund would be used to “aid in providing at least a seven 
months’ minimum term for all schools;” and two, that it 
was to “assist in the promotion of equalization of educa- 
tional opportunity for all children in the public elemen- 
tary and high schools.” These provisions are present in 
Appendices 5-5 State Board of Education Regulations for 
the Annual Apportionment of the Minimum Program 
Fund for the Fiscal Year Beginning October 1, 1987, and 
Appendix 5-6, Statutory Provisions for the Apportion- 
ment of Education Funds to Independent Cities. 


Additional Educational Legislation, 1935 
The Minimum Program Acts were adopted by the Leg- 
islature without a dissenting vote. G.S. Smith, president 


of the Alabama Education Association, praised Governor 
Graves and the legislature for their efforts. He asserted 


A-259 


that the “passage of the Minimum Program Act of 1935 
marked the greatest single step yet taken to insure equi- 
table distribution of public funds for education” (Ala- 
bama School Journal, Voi LIV, May 1937, p. 19). 


The School Budget Act, 1935. An additional act, the 
School Budget Act, was also passed on September 2 
1935, to extend the budgetary procedures imposed on 
state government by the Budget and Financial Control 
Act of 1932 to the operation of local school systems. It 
provided that each of the 67 county boards of education 
and the 45 city boards of education could not spend 
more for current expenses in any year than its revenue 
receipts for that year plus balances on hand. This guaran- 


teed that every school board would live within its j 
ts 
(Acts 1935, No. 300, p. 728). ithin its income 


The School Warrant Act, 1935. The School Warrant Act, 
also passed on September 2, 1935, enabled local boards 
of education to clear up their floating debts by repealing 
all old school warrant laws and placing restrictions on 
refunding warrants. Authority was granted to refund any 


warrant at interest rates over four and one-half 
(Acts 1935, No. 302, p. 735). ail percent 


The School Textbook Act, 1935. The School Textbook 
Act was approved earlier on July 17, 1935, to provide for 
state-owned textbooks for the first, second, and third 
grades. An initial appropriation was provided in the 
amount of $175,000 from any funds in the treasury not 
otherwise appropriated under the direction of the gover- 
nor. The revenues to accomplish this were to come from 
a levy on tobacco products (Acts 1935, No. 201, p. 594). 


A-260 
Emergency Salary Appropriations, 1935. A special salary 


appropriation to provide funds to pay public elementary 
and high school teachers for the remainder of the nor- 
mal 1934-35 school term was also enacted by the legisla- 
ture. For those county and city school systems below 
5,000 in population, and the state secondary agricultural 
schools, where all local, state, and federal funds available 
had been exhausted for salaries, the sum of $500,000 was 
appropriated from the state treasury to the State Board 
of Education. An additional stipulation was that no 
teacher would receive more than $60 per month for a 
maximum of a seven-month elementary school term or a 
nine-month high school term (Acts 1935, No. 212, p. 


600). 


The Sales Tax Enacted, 1936 


The financial troubles of public education in Alabama 
were not over. Appropriation and apportionment plans 
meant nothing without adequate revenues. 


The School Crisis and Proration of 1935-37. 


In spite of the increased school appropriation 
and other constructive school laws enacted in 
1935 which had wide-spread influence, revenue 
receipts were insufficient to pay the appropria- 
tions. This led to proration and a school crisis 
which required additional legislation. The 
needed legislation was not passed until a second 
special session of the legislature was called, at 
which time the necessary measures were taken, 
and the schools returned to normal (Cain, p. 


233).

The governor called attention to this last plank in his 
platform and noted that it would be impossible to oper- 
ate the schools without further taxation in their behalf. 
Governor Graves proposed two tax changes: exemp- 
tion of homesteads from the 6.5-mill state tax and other 
ad valorem taxes, and a luxury tax to fund education and 
replace revenues lost through the exemption. Graves 
continued his rejection of the general sales tax, believing 
it unfair because it taxed the necessities of life such as 
bread, shoes, and nails. He asked, instead, for a luxury 
tax on everything that is not a necessity. In addition, he 
suggested that the legislature increase the franchise tax 
on foreign corporations and consider taxes on natural 
gas, export power, and amusements of all kinds (Graves, 
February 11, 1936, Extra Session, p. IX). 


A-263 


This tax measure was soundly defeated in the legisla- 
ture. The session ended after 25 legislative days, with 
Graves taking the blame: 


The people are thumbs down on a spending 
spree-they want thoughtful and conservative 
spending putting money where it will do the 
most good at the least outlay. 

Governor Graves should learn a lesson from 
“back home” that there is a limit to the patience 
and endurance of the people Staggering under 
debt and the aftermath of depression (Monigom- 
ery Weekly, 1936). 


The Special Legislative Session of November 23, 1936. 


Graves learned well from his defeat in the first extra ses- 


sion and immediately began planning for the second ex- 
tra session. On November 23, 1936, he presented a sec- 
ond plan to the legislature: 


1. To revise the Revenue Laws and to provide 
additional revenue for the schools and to en- 
able the State, County and City governments 
to function adequately. 

2. To provide for the payment of all educa- 
tional activities, experimental and extension 
services, out of the Alabama Special Educa- 
tional Trust Fund and to relieve the General 
Fund of all such payments. 

3. To provide for an appropriation from the 
General Fund to take care of any deficit in 
the Alabama Special Educational Trust Fund. 

4. To provide for the exemption from State ad 
valorem taxes of homesteads, as defined by 
the Constitution and Laws of the State of Ala- 
bama. 

5. To set up a “Property Tax Relief Fund” and 
reduce State ad valorem taxes. 


A-264 


6. To regulate the manufacture and sale of 
spiritous, vinous or malt beverages through 
State owned and operated stores of other 
State supervision, and to provide a referen- 
dum thereon to the electors of Alabama 
(Graves, November 23, 1936, Second Extra 
Session, p. 11). 


Graves also presented to the legislature the Zukoski 
Report, a study of school finance which had been pre- 
pared by a group of Birmingham businessmen headed by 
Charles F. Zukoski, Trust Officer and Vice President of 
the First National Bank of Birmingham. This group had 
come to Montgomery at the request of Senator Hays 
Tucker of Tuscaloosa and anti-administration legislators 
to study funding for public education. After spending 
three weeks in the State Department of Education, the 
group acknowledged the crisis in educational finance 
and confirmed that conditions were being accurately pre- 
sented to the legislature and to the public. The report 
recommended an additional 3.0 mill tax to be included 
as a part of the Minimum Program (Graves, November 
23, 1936, p. VIII). 

Governor Graves recommended the enactment of an 
excise tax to be known as a privilege licensing tax on 
retail merchandising of personal property; the measure 
of the tax was to be one and one-half percent of the gross 
receipts of the merchandising business, including amuse- 
ments run for pay. He projected that this tax would yield 
some $4.5 million the first year in new revenues. He 
urged that the returns from this license tax be placed in 
the ASETF to replace the amount that it takes to exempt 
homesteads from the 6.5-mill state tax. He asked that 
every educational and extension program from the Gen- 
eral Fund be transferred to the ASETF and that an addi- 
tional one million dollars be transferred from the Gen- 
eral Fund to the ASETF. He also called for a bill estab- 


A-265 


lishing the Property Tax Relief Fund with the surplus in 
the ASETF to be transferred to it at the end of each year 
to further reduce the ad valorem tax (Graves, November 
23, 1936, pp. XX-XXI). 


Gross Receipts Tax Act, 1936. The legislature responded 
favorably to Governor Graves’ proposals and approved 
the Gross Receipts Act on December 17, 1936: 


Section 1. Every person, firm, corporation, as- 
sociation, Or co-partnership opening, establish- 
ing, Operating, or maintaining one or more re- 
tail stores or retail mercantile establishments 
within the State, whether under one general 
management or not, shall, in addition to all 
other licenses or taxes of whatever kind now 
levied by law, pay to the State of Alabama, as a 
license or privilege tax for the privilege of doing 
such business, an amount equal to one and one- 
half per cent (1-4%) on the gross sales of said 
business. 

Section 3..... Upon every person, firm, cor- 
poration, association, or co-partnership en- 
gaged or continuing within this State in the 
business of conducting places of amusement or 
entertainment, billiard and pool rooms, bowl-

ture shows, vaudevilles, amusement parks, 
athletic contests, including wrestling and prize 
fights, boxing exhibitions, football and baseball 
games, skating rinks, race tracks, or any other 
place at which entertainment is offered to the 
public, including public bathing places, public 
dance halls of every kind and description within 
the State of Alabama, a license tax in addition 
to all other taxes now or hereafter levied, in an 
amount equal to one and one-half per cent 
(1-’%,) of the gross receipts of such business or 
places of amusement.

End of Proration, 1937 


In 1936-37, for the first time in five years, one hundred 
percent of the appropriations due to education were 
paid, largely because of the consequence of earmarking a 
major portion of the proceeds of the sales tax for public 
education. State Superintendent J.A. Keller commented 
on this event: 


A-274 


I am aware of the fact that the practice of 
earmarking revenue is opposed by many politi- 
cal science experts. But in view of the rulings of 
our Supreme Court, which protect earmarked 
funds but give education last place in the State 
General Fund, we have no alternative (Annual 
Report, 1935-37, Part 1, p. 24). 


* * * 


Summary 


The Alabama Special Educational Trust Fund has 
served to insulate public education from two types of 
financial failures in Alabama. The first is that since access 
to the local property tax — the usual source of local 
school funds nationwide — is so constrained by constitu- 
tional provisions, the only substantial source of funds is 
those from the state. In the case of the ASETF, the two 
growth taxes of the state — the income tax and the sales 
tax — are earmarked for “educational purposes only.” 
Therefore, the only consistent and elastic source of rev- 
enues in the entire state has been provided for public 
schools. 

The second is that since public education is an non- 
essential function of state government, only the earmark- 
ing of funds through a separate fund — the ASETF — 
has buffered public K-12 schools from the uncertainties 
surrounding proration of the General Fund. Simply put, 
the public schools of this state could not have achieved 
the present successful status, although inadequate, with- 
out protection from discriminatory proration. A constitu- 
tional guarantee of public education is lacking in Ala- 
bama. The existence of the ASETF does provide the ap- 
pearance of such a constitutional guarantee. 


A-275 


The ASETF and public has naturally come to dominate 
the activities ofthe legislature for several reasons. First, 
because the ASETF has usured the two major tax re- 
sources of the state, it represents both the largest con- 
tinuing and growth revenues of the state. This results in 
the continual threat of diversion for other purposes. Sec- 
ondly, no function of state government requires the mag- 
nitude of expenditure as does public education. The cast- 
ing of this expenditure promotes extensive discussion 
and deliberation as existing and new programs by the 
various consumers of the ASETF compete for attention. 
Thirdly, no program of state government has such an 
impact on so many lives as does public education. The 
political reality of thisampact is expressed through the 
annual appropriation from the ASETF. 

It is for these and other reasons that each session of 
the legislature tends to devote itself in substantial part to 
the ASETF and the appropriations therefrom for public 
education. Tracing the ASETF through these sessions re- 
flects in many ways the politics, policies, and aspirations 
of Alabama since 1927. Our public policy toward public 
education has been set in these appropriations. There is 
no reason to expect any difference in the future.
